And lastly, we'll go into a discussion about atrial fibrillation clinic. So atrial fibrillation, again, is the most common arrhythmia, and it's an irregularly irregular rhythm. So on the left of your screen, you see kind of a normal conduction system in the heart electrical system in the heart, where the heartbeat is initiated in the top right chamber of the heart from the sinus node. This is an electrical bundle of cells. And electrical impulses are then conducted throughout the top right chamber to this another place called AV node, which is a bundle of cells that conducts electricity from the top chambers of the heart to the bottom chambers of the heart. And electrical impulses are also conducted from the right top chamber to the left top chamber of the heart. What happens in atrial fibrillation is that there are some spots in the, mostly in the top left chamber of the heart, where the veins from the lung, the pulmonary veins join in and connect with the top left chamber of the heart. And those cells can generate electrical impulses, which in a chaotic manner. And that's what initiates atrial fibrillation. And then it propagates irregularly irregular rhythm in the whole heart. And this is kind of a small animation of just to visualize how the top chambers, instead of contracting, they are querying. And, you know, what causes atrial fibrillation is the kind of big question. We don't understand fully a lot of the causes, but the most important risk factor is aging. So as we age and, you know, have growth and changes in the heart, the normal cells get replaced sometimes with the abnormal cells. And that is causing atrial fibrillation to happen more often as we get older. The other contributing factors is genetics and some genes that we may be born with. And if atrial fibrillation is detected before age 45, that's when we are thinking that genes may be some of the contributing factors why a person develops atrial fibrillation early on. And we usually recommend to do genetic testing at that stage. The other factors include lifestyle factors, such as, you know, being overweight and lack of exercise. We'll talk about all of this later on, high blood pressure, sleep apnea, and alcohol is a kind of one of the major lifestyle factors along with smoking. And medical conditions. Sometimes if people are very sick, those things can contribute to atrial fibrillation, such as having thyroid abnormalities, or being really sick, you know, having infection or other diseases, or having surgery of different types, that can temporarily increase the risk of atrial fibrillation. And then I'm not going to go into varied details of this busy slide, but, you know, there are, again, changes at the cellular level inside the heart in this top left chamber that leads to heart being susceptible for initiating and then propagating atrial fibrillation. It's very common. So if we leave, you know, long enough, one in four to five people will develop atrial fibrillation over a lifetime. And it's projected that the number of people with atrial fibrillation is going to increase over time as we have, you know, aging population and the comorbidities that are associated with aging. You know, AFib, it's associated, the reason we are worried about it in addition to, you know, having symptoms, and we'll cover symptoms, is that it increases risk of stroke, which needs management to decrease the risk of stroke, it's associated with increased risk of heart attack, weak heart muscle, increased hospitalizations. So we, that this is a reason why we put in efforts to decrease these complications associated with AFib. One of the important things to understand in an atrial fibrillation is a progressive disease. So it starts from being just at risk, which is many of us, you know, when you have high blood pressure, when you have diabetes, and, you know, any comorbidities, that those things increase, puts us at risk of developing atrial fibrillation. Then some of us develop symptoms, symptoms such as, you know, few extra heartbeats or runs of extra heartbeats. And those are signs that AFib is about to develop, but it's not there yet. It's called pre-atrial fibrillation. And then finally, we have diagnosis of atrial fibrillation. And permanent atrial fibrillation is if you with your doctor decide that, you know, there is no more, you know, you're not going to try to get back to normal rhythm. And we'll talk about some of the times that that can be, that can be the solution. So types of atrial fibrillation. So atrial fibrillation types are paroxysmal and persistent. Those are just fancy terms. Paroxysmal means at atrial fibrillation that comes and goes. And it, you know, every time it comes on, it lasts less than seven days. And then it goes into persistent atrial fibrillation when, you know, atrial fibrillation happens more than seven days. You know, sometimes atrial fibrillation starts with that paroxysmal episodes when the AFib comes and goes, and then it progresses to persistent atrial fibrillation. So the more atrial fibrillation is in the heart, is happening in the heart, the normal cells are getting replaced with abnormal cells and it propagates more atrial fibrillation. So it's a vicious cycle. The more AFib you have, the more likely that it's going to stay and then heart getting harder to control. And then longstanding persistent, if you is, if AFib is present more than one year, and then permanent is, as I mentioned earlier, when we are deciding, it's not like a physiologic state, but we're deciding that we're not going to restore the normal rhythm. AFib symptoms are very, you know, variable. It's very individual. Everyone feels AFib differently, and some people don't have any symptoms at all. Feeling tired, usually not having as much energy is a very common symptom. Not being able to do things that we used to do before, having heart racing, shortness of breath, or chest discomfort, some of the other symptoms that people may have. But again, it's varies widely among different people. So now, how do we make diagnosis of atrial fibrillation? So we need to have a documented tracing, or like the tracing of the heart activation that confirms that there is atrial fibrillation. So it's either done by electrocardiogram, or heart monitors. Sometimes people wear those patch monitors that can pick up atrial fibrillation. Sometimes people have implanted devices like pacemakers or defibrillators that has a, you know, electrical wire in the top chamber of the heart, and that can detect atrial fibrillation. And more, you know, recently, smart technologies, such as smart devices that you may wear with your wrist, or, you know, have sensors on the back of the small device that can record the electrocardiogram. The other, you know, the other way of detecting atrial fibrillation is this photo flood ismography, which is not an electrocardiogram. This is, you know, any wearable devices like watches, they emit lights, and then they measure the change in the pulse wave into the, in the wrist as the blood is flowing. And then it detects whether this is regular, as in the top tracing here, or it is irregular, as in the bottom tracing there. As you can imagine, there can be many factors while this may look irregular. So there's, you know, all these manufacturers that went through the FDA clearance for this type of devices, went through rigorous testing and algorithm development to make sure that their, their kind of algorithms are accurate for detecting atrial fibrillation, but this is not like, you know, 100%. And I will show you, you know, this is just quickly, like, you know, huge study where people who are just wearing an Apple watch, half million people, about half percent get not got notification that they had atrial fibrillation. And eventually, out of those people who got the notification, only 34% got actual diagnosis of atrial fibrillation on the patch monitor that they were wearing for seven days. Now, that doesn't mean that it was all falsely positive, but it means that, you know, that they may not be having AFib episode when they were wearing the patch monitor. And sometimes this can be this, you know, we have to follow up with the actual recording of the electrocardiogram to make the AFib diagnosis. And this is a very similar study with a Fitbit. And the kind of same result, 1% out of almost half a million patients got the notification and 32% got diagnosed officially with the atrial fibrillation afterwards. And then this is reiterating the comment that if you get an irregular rhythm notification on the watch to contact the physician for the more test, for more testing. And the other tools that these wearable technologies have are actual tracings of the ECGs. And these can be reviewed by clinician. I will point your attention to this in conclusive tracings. There's a lot of times, you know, that the tracing is not good enough to interpret the signal because of the noise. And it's not always that it tells you that it's AFib. It's truly AFib and it tells you it's sinus rhythm, it's sinus rhythm. So there is definitely a value of examining the tracings by the clinician. And moving on to the pillars of atrial fibrillation management. So the main three goals whenever we get diagnosis of atrial fibrillation is to focus on stroke prevention, risk factor modification, how we can prevent the progression of atrial fibrillation, and then symptom management. So first, stroke prevention. It's very important to protect the brain. And the stroke is brain damage, which is caused by the blood flow not going to the brain. So what happens is that when the top chambers are fibrillating, the blood clot can form and that blood clot can move to the brain and cause stroke or any other organs and cause the damage there. So what the options are, either blood thinners, pills, or sometimes people may be eligible for procedure where this stroke prevention can be performed through the procedure. But it depends on your individual risks. And the risk of stroke is assessed by the CHADS-VASC score. On the right of the screen, you see the list of medical conditions, which gives us ability to calculate the annual risk of stroke. On the left of the screen, there is a picture of the heart that is cut out. And you see this kind of a pouch that is coming out. It's called left atrial appendage. And it's like an appendix that we have in the intestines. And this is the area where with atrial fibrillation, there's a slow flow in there and the blood clots can form. So, and your physician will, or clinician, will calculate your specific stroke risk and balance it with your leading risk to make a decision about initiating the blood thinners for stroke prevention. And one quick study that I will highlight is that we're currently doing a study at Johns Hopkins. We are one of the sites for this large randomized study where you can get an Apple Watch directed anticoagulation. So if you have infrequent atrial fibrillation episodes, the watch can monitor for presence of atrial fibrillation and only take it for 30 days around the time when atrial fibrillation is experienced. And the other group is going to take blood thinners normally as they would. And then we'll have an answer hopefully in a few years on whether this is going to be an effective approach Dr. Dr. Dr. Dr.! Dr. 도! 도! 도 도 도 atrial fibrillation recurrence was less and lower, 60% versus 80% among people who did this exercise program and achieved the 210 minutes of exercise per week. And this was also another study of addressing risk factors such as exercise, weight loss, alcohol reduction, smoking cessation after catheter ablation. And I'll talk to you what catheter ablation is, a procedure to treat atrial fibrillation. If you did this program for a risk factor modification, you had better outcomes compared to usual care in terms of recurrence of atrial fibrillation. This is also very strong evidence about alcohol. So there was a study of 140 adults who were drinking more than 10 standard drinks per week. And if you compare the group who was recommended to us in the group of abstaining from alcohol completely for six months versus the people who continued drinking, there was significant reduction in atrial fibrillation recurrence among those who stopped drinking. So overall, these studies have led, and many others have led to recommendations that weight loss of 10%, alcohol reduction or abstinence, we usually say less than two to three drinks per week, smoking cessation, 210 minutes of moderate intensity exercise, controlling your blood pressure, and screening for sleep apnea and treatment are important to prevent, help improve outcomes of atrial fibrillation. And we, you know, it's hard to make behavior changes, and we understand that. And this is why we did some sessions with the patients with atrial fibrillation and their health partners to brainstorm on how, what are the ways how we can help modify this risk factors. And we developed this virtual program, leveraging education and tracking of the medications and health metrics with the smartphone app, as well as smartwatch, black pressure monitor scale, and virtual coaching. And the study is currently in progress at Hopkins, where we are evaluating the efficacy of this and reducing atrial fibrillation recurrence after ablation. So stay tuned for results. In terms of the last pillar, but very important is symptom management. And for most, this means restoring or maintaining normal heart rhythm. And there's a few ways how we can do this. So how we can maintain the normal rhythm is enterorhythmic medications and catheter ablation, where cardioversion is also one of the ways if someone is in AFib for prolonged period of time, more than seven days, or they have very significant symptoms, even for a few days, it can be a short term kind of a band-aid solution. So enterorhythmic medications, they are different types and different classes of enterorhythmic medications. They all come with their own contraindications, what are the health conditions that you cannot use them for, and they usually have a few different side effects. So it's very individualized, you know, what which enterorhythmic medications people may be eligible for. Next is our catheter ablation, which is a procedure that is done in the electrophysiology lab. We put the big IVs in the vein, in the leg, and through those big IVs, put the catheters all the way up to the heart. And you can see on this picture on the right, this is a picture of the top left chamber of the heart. You can see the veins, the red, depicted in red, coming from the lungs to the heart. And around those veins, as I mentioned earlier, those usually the triggers for atrial fibrillation. So we put our catheters there to deliver electrical impulses. Currently, you know, most of us are doing this with a pulse field ablation, which is a new kind of approach for delivering electrical impulses to create holes in the cells that are causing atrial fibrillation and get rid of them. And then we create an electrical map of the heart after we do the ablation to make sure that those areas are now silent and are not able to generate electrical impulses anymore. That, you know, there's no cure to atrial fibrillation per se, meaning, you know, atrial fibrillation ablation success rate is about 70% at one year for people with a paroxysmal AFib, the AFib that comes and goes, and 60% among people with a paroxysmal AFib that is a persistent type, but is more effective than medications and making, you know, increasing chances of being free from atrial fibrillation. And then the cardioversion is a procedure where the pads are placed on the chest, you know, a patient is put to sleep, and electrical impulses is delivered to restart the heart basically. And that leads to restoration of normal rhythm. You never know, depending on how long a person has had AFib, what comorbidities are present, that, you know, there may be a recurrence immediately or, you know, a few months later, you know, or down the road even farther. But this is not a, like, this is not a fix. This is kind of a more of a band-aid intervention. I do want to highlight one of the studies and, you know, I will, trust me, I will explain this, you know, busy slide. But this was a big, big study that was conducted in multiple centers about 2,700 patients who had atrial fibrillation diagnosed within the last 12 months. And they compared if, you know, the rhythm was controlled, early rhythm control, which was done with the antiarrhythmic drugs or ablation. And then control group just got medications to control the heart rate. And after five years, the trial was actually stopped because restoring normal rhythm with either medications or ablation reduced the outcome of, you know, death from the heart issues, stroke, weak heart muscle, admissions to the hospital from weak heart muscle or heart attack. So, this was a big study where, okay, so, you know, restoring normal rhythm is important. And then the second important study was the one comparing whether you do this with ablation versus medications. And this was among patients with paroxysmal atrial fibrillation. And what they found that if you did ablation versus antiarrhythmic drugs, the risk of recurrence of atrial fibrillation at one year was much lower in the group that had ablation than antiarrhythmic, who took antiarrhythmic medications. And similarly, the risk of progression from paroxysmal to persistent atrial fibrillation was less in the group that received ablation compared to the medications alone. And there were more side effects in the group that received medications. And then kind of last but not least, you know, some people are not eligible for rhythm control or restoring normal rhythm for one reason or the other. So, how we can control the heart rate is with medications. Our last resort, if, you know, sometimes people have multiple ablations of medications or AFib for a very long time, we can put wires, you know, pacemaker, basically wires in the top chamber of the heart, bottom chamber of the heart on the right and left side, and disconnect the connection between electrical connection between the top and bottom chambers of the heart. So, that doesn't get rid of AFib. You know, the top chambers are still in AFib, but it prevents heart rate to be high. So, that may be a solution for some. Okay. Okay. === FILE: Bone Health of Women with CF Across the Age Spectrum [WwVOZYWvZ_c].txt ===

 nassititititit. Hello. My name is Melinda Wu. I'm a pediatric endocrinologist at Johns Hopkins. At the 2025 North American Cystic Fibrosis Conference, I had the privilege of discussing women's health, specifically bone health. Other colleagues discussed CF specific concerns for pregnancy, lactation and menopause. People with cystic fibrosis are at increased risk for osteoporosis at a young age. Some studies of people with CF have shown that children do not accrue bone mass as quickly as healthy peers and that young adults are not maintaining their bone mass as their healthy peers are doing. There are many factors affecting the risk for and timing of when a person may develop bone disease. There is little data so far about contraceptive method and bone health for women with CF. In healthy women, the use of depoprogesterone injections can prevent bone accrual. In other populations at risk for osteoporosis, combined oral contraceptive pills may not provide sufficient estrogen for bone accrual. In our previous single center cross-sectional study, the use of contraception with progesterone and estrogen was associated with lower bone density. In our follow-up study, we found that the age at which estrogen-containing contraception was started affected the trajectory of bone density. At NACFC, I was able to present our recent findings from the case for CF study. The case for CF study is a cross-sectional study of self-reported contraception use since their first period in women with cystic fibrosis. These survey results from 551 women were then matched to national longitudinal patient registry data spanning 13 years. These participants were recruited from 10 sites, including Johns Hopkins. Overall, these women who participated were relatively healthy and representative of women in the United States with cystic fibrosis. They were young adults, majority white, taking CFTR modulators with normal BMI, as well as good lung function defined as greater than 70% predicted FEV1. First, we compared the registry data and a manual chart review of one-fifth of the participants. When comparing the registry-reported osteopenia and osteoporosis to low bone density as measured by DEXA, there was an 81% agreement between the registry and manual chart review. Then, we performed univariable and multivariable Cox proportional hazards models to examine the association of risk factors and developing bone disease in the following year. In univariable analysis, increasing age, lower BMI, lower FEV1, and the use of progesterone-only contraception were each significantly associated with an increased hazard of developing bone disease in the following year. However, when we did multivariable analysis, adjusting for age, BMI, FEV1, and contraceptive method, we found that only increasing age and lower BMI remained significant. But the case for CF study was specifically looking at the association of contraception and the risk of developing bone disease. And so, we followed up on our univariable result that the use of progesterone-only contraception methods was associated with an increased hazard of developing bone disease in the following year. And we specifically split out the use of systemic progesterone, like depoprogesterone injections, from locoprogesterone, like a progesterone-eluting intrauterine device, IUD. And then, we found that systemic progesterone was associated with an increased hazard of developing bone disease, like in the general population, but this did not reach statistical significance. Part of why we think that this result may be true is because participants in this study changed their contraceptive method of choice. And few participants persisted with any single contraceptive method for more than four years at a time, although they may have switched back and forth between different contraceptive methods. This was also a retrospective study relying on self-report and the recall from 13 years of prior medication use. And a thank you to my colleagues and collaborators at Johns Hopkins and Emory University and everybody in the Case for CF Osteoporosis group.

=== FILE: Childhood and Adolescent Obesity ｜ Q&A [yocobNHPa_g].txt ===

 The Fit and Healthy Kids Clinic is a clinic at Kennedy Krieger Institute that's multidisciplinary that was designed to treat kids that either have elevated BMI above the 95th percent of the average, ambul ambul ambul ambul ambulier ambulier ambulier ambulier ambulier ambulier ambulier ambul!! concern or that the BMI is already over the 95th percentile. Most of the kids and young adults that come and see us do have a cognitive or physical difference like the ones we spoke about, but it's not an absolute requirement. We're welcome to all children or young adults that need to come and see us. So the treatment options that we have for either rapidly rising weight or elevated weight in childhood is actually pretty broad. Sometimes the treatment is just lifestyle alone. And in the patients that we treat at Kennedy Krieger Institute, the lifestyle changes can actually be very complicated, which is why it requires a dedicated team that has experience with this population. Other times we do use medications and some of the pharmacotherapy that is typically associated with treating adolescent or young children with elevated weights. And those medications are at least five to six. It really depends. Sometimes it's not about adding a medication, but removing one or changing one that is actually promoting weight gain. The other parts of treatment involve whole health, right? So how is sleep? Do we have sleep at the end that needs to be addressed? Are we getting the right number of sleep hours? Sometimes it's about movement. Are we getting enough movement during the day? And so that's part of the lifestyle recommendations as well. And then other parts are about nutrition. How many in a child who, for instance, is only getting G-tube feeds? Can we modify the nutrition that they're getting through the G-tube? Or in somebody who has very picky eating, can we modify some of the types of foods that we're getting? And can that shift something? And sometimes it's just about basic education of what good nutrition is that may shift the curve. So what is the healthful eating activity and weight program? The healthful eating activity and weight program is a clinical program dedicated to supporting healthy living for individuals who have overweight or obesity. And in this program, we work one-on-one with patients. We also have group visits. And we try to develop a comprehensive strategy to support each individual's health goals. And this may involve multiple areas of support. So we encompass lifestyle interventions such as nutrition, physical activity, looking at behavioral health interventions, as well as sleep. And then we also may incorporate medications as part of our management. So our team is primarily comprised of obesity medicine specialists. These are individuals who have completed additional training in the pathophysiology and treatment of obesity. In addition to that, we collaborate very closely with bariatric surgeons, as well as gastroenterology to offer different types of treatment modalities, including endobariatrics and bariatric surgery. We also work closely with nutrition through dietitians, as well as behavioral health with clinical psychologists as needed. And lastly, we also collaborate with hepatology and endocrinology as two commonly used specialties. So our program has patients who are 16 years and older. And generally, our patients are looking to achieve some form of weight loss support. And they may also have some coexisting medical conditions as well. So children with disabilities are at a higher risk of being overweight because they have different needs than other kids. That's one of the biggest factors. So sometimes that looks like different feeding patterns or different nutrition plans or pickiness of eating. The textures or colors or taste of foods may be very sensitive to that. And so that needs to be adjusted. Food as a love language is very important in this population too, like most of the population. But sometimes it may be the only real connection time between the caregivers and the kids. And so sometimes that has to be addressed. The physical activity needs are different of this population. They may be sensitive to large crowds or sensitive to sweating or they don't like a way a certain activity makes them feel. So they're less likely to do it. So the general inherent physical activity that some kids have by going out and playing with their friends or playing on a sports team may be different in this population. So their activity may be lower. There may be more screen time because that might be a way to modify behavior or to reward good behavior as the screen time. And so sometimes the screen time is higher, which means less activity. The sleep may be more abnormal than typical. So sleep apnea may be more common. Some kids have central sleep apnea. Some kids, because of the way that their brain works, it's hard for them to fall asleep or they wake up early. So they get less hours of sleep, which changes the metabolism. And then finally, a lot of these kids are on medications that promote weight gain. And that makes it really challenging to be at a healthy weight if you're on a medication that promotes weight gain or elevated sugars or increased eating. So all of those things have to be addressed during the clinic visit. So overweight or obesity are conditions that we have come to understand as truly complex and multifactorial. There are many drivers and one of the primary drivers for individuals may be genetics. But then you layer on top of someone's baseline genetic predisposition that they're living in a specific environment that is going to have specific factors like what type of food access, cultural preferences, knowledge do they have. And then in addition, like their neighborhood environment, how physically active are their parents or their siblings, that environment, right? And then all of the trajectories and things that happen in life for that person. And so you essentially have, you know, someone's baseline genetic composition or their genes, and then you have the environment in which they live in and they grow up in. And if you combine both of those things together, we have this concept known as epigenetics where, you know, there's a predisposition and then there's environment altogether, you know, there's something that develops over time. And so we can end up seeing that somebody who perhaps is initially predisposed to weight gain, you know, in an environment where they have access to more, you know, processed food that are a little bit higher in calories, lower in nutrient density, less access to physical activity, higher stress levels. You know, these are just examples of drivers. And that person over time can then, you know, manifest with overweight and obesity. That's one example of a scenario, but gives an, you know, just an idea of how this is really a complex problem. So overweight and obesity have traditionally been defined by something known as body mass index, or BMI. And this essentially is a measure of somebody's height as measured by their surface area and their weight. And so someone's height and weight is put into this calculation that is their body mass index. And we don't need to go into the full history, but essentially these thresholds have been used at a population level to be able to predict risk of early mortality and also early morbidity. And morbidity just refers to having some conditions or complications of health that are related to that weight gain or that increase in body mass index over time. I will say there's a lot of nuance that is now, you know, I think more readily understood and hopefully incorporated into medical practice beyond just that BMI measurement. But by strict BMI criteria, there are specific kind of thresholds of 25 to 29 for non-South Asians, so everyone else. That would classify someone as having overweight and then above 30 as classifying someone as having obesity. Now, with all of that said, there are subsequent classification schemes that have been created to really better further define someone's actual health status and health risks. So a referring practitioner, whether a primary care doctor or a subspecialist, should understand that obesity is a complex disease and multifactorial and lives within some sort of a home environment. And so an understanding the part of the family and the child is very important before making a referral. I think sometimes referrals are made, you know, with the intention of doing something good for a child. But if a child and or their family is really not ready to commit to working on something that is hard and is a long journey, then it's not they're not going to be successful in it. And so I think having that, you know, really honest kind of discussion with the family and making sure that they understand, like, this is why we're doing this. We really want to support your kid developing in a way that's healthy for the rest of their life and support healthy habits and just a healthy growth trajectory for them. That's really the intention. The intention is not fixating on a specific number that's perfect. It's really all around kind of health.

 n cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater cater for Johns Hopkins Medicine and your host for today's episode, along with my awesome co-host, Dr. Carolyn Kumstee Fowler, who is Executive Director for Nursing Wellbeing here at Johns Hopkins. And it is such a pleasure to be here together. Carolyn, you haven't done this in a little while. Oh, you haven't. Hello, my friend. And I know how much we love getting together to talk about how we impact well-being. So I'm delighted to be with you today. Delighted to be able to share this conversation with our listeners. So, a hot topic for us, well, really a big hot topic for the last year has been just a lot of change and a lot of transitions. There have been changes. We're in academic medicine, changes in academic medicine with funding policies and priorities, changes in thinking about how healthcare, things like Medicare and Medicaid are funded and those approaches, especially here in Maryland. And there's just a lot to be said about that. And I would love sort of, you know, you've taught in the past on change and transition. I'd love to hear some of your reflections on the principles that apply in this space and how we can use those moving forward to effectively embrace the new year. Wonderful. Well, let's just for a moment separate the fact that there are two big components of this. So the one big component is that when we have rapid change, there are tactical and logistical things that we need to pay attention to, which is a whole different conversation. And then there's the human response to change and transition. And I think that's really what we're talking about because it's that human response that can feel so hard for us and can lead to challenges to our wellbeing. So I'm not going to share with you anything sort of earth shatteringly new, but rather some work from William Bridges. Now, William Bridges wrote a Sentinel book on change and transition about 35 years ago. And the reason I like his work is because he's very clear to distinguish between what changes and what transition is. And we talk a lot about managing change or living through change, but actually it's not the change we're living through. So a change is a moment in time, something happens or it stops happening. For example, when we're bereaved or we've finished grad school or we get married or have children, there's a date on which those things happen. And that's a very clear change from before specific time point to after the change. Right. It's a very clear before and after. What Bridges described is that there is a physical change, which is before and after, but there is also a period of psychological adjustment where what we're being asked to do is to transition from a place of where we understood ourselves in relationship to one state of being or one set of conditions. And we're now being asked to transition to the reality of living in a different set of conditions. And what's important about this, firstly, is that it's frightening and it's stressful. And we have to acknowledge that and not downplay it. I think a lot of us want to get busy and fix things and do things and say, well, the other stuff will take care of itself. No, it won't. We like to distract ourselves. We do indeed. We love to distract ourselves. And I also, I think sometimes the temptation for leaders is to get busy with the logistical things because firstly, they're important to take care of. Secondly, they're kind of, they're, they're clear. Like I know what I have to do. Whereas dealing with the psychological response to transition, it's messy. It's, it's more of that emotional response stuff that we heard when we were teaching crisis leadership, how people say that's the piece that feels stressful because it's something we're not used to dealing with. So in this period of transition that Bridges calls the neutral zone, we see several things happening. We see people's motivation dropping off. We see people's engagement dropping off. We see people's, you know, collaboration and energy dropping off. And it's so easy for us to try and manage that tactically. Like we just need you to produce more or do more or being here more. But what we're being invited to do is to say, what is the lived experience of our colleagues when they are living through this transition? And what do they need to help them firstly come to terms with where we're journeying to? So about one part talking to three parts listening. But it's hard, it's hard to be a good listener. But it's just one part of the communication equation, and it's perhaps the less important part, especially in times of transition and change, etc. It's so interesting. I couldn't agree with you more. And I think one of the challenges to becoming a good listener and becoming an effective listener is that we are so busy, we have so much going on in our brain, that while we are allegedly listening to what people are talking to us about, we're processing it. We're processing it to either disagree with it, or to try and fix it, or to respond to it. What we're seldom doing is listening to understand. And even when people will say things to us that we might not understand, it's so easy for us to sort of fill in the gap with an assumption. I think that's what they're telling. As opposed to saying, what is it that you mean by that? Now, I did a podcast with Dr. Deb Baker, our chief nursing executive, and she was talking about the impact of a question that I shared with her during the pandemic. And that is sort of the, the, and what else question. I was just going to ask you about that. Tell us about that. Tell us about that. Well, this is the work of Michael Bungie, Stania, his question, that this question comes from him. But essentially, one of the things I realized is that we have come into a helping profession. Every one of us who works in healthcare came into this profession to help people. And so we, we want to help, we want to fix, we want to solve, we want to reduce people's suffering. And that sets us up for trouble when it comes to being a great listener. Because if I am listening with the intent to fix something, or to help you, or to provide an answer to you, or help you find some certainty, then what I am doing is I am not actually unpacking what it is you're wanting to say to me. And so what Michael Bungie, Stania recommends is this question called the, the all question or the, and what else? So when somebody is saying like, this is what's troubling me, you might say, and what else? And gradually, you're going to repeat that, and what else several times. Now, not to be repetitive, there are different ways of doing it. But essentially, what we're inviting people to do is to just keep talking and reflecting on what's going on with them. And at some point, the sort of, it's almost like you're taking the pressure out of a pressure cooker. Offloading the tension. Yeah. Right. That's kind of, it's coming off. Because I've always said the time to ask what's going on is not when you try and take, like, we're not going to take the lid off a pressure cooker when it's blowing steam. That's just a disaster waiting to happen. So we want the pressure to come down, then we can take the lid off and look inside and see what's going on. And the same is true when people are in this position. I love that analogy. Right. That we want to be able to give them enough space that they can get some of this steam, the anxiety, whatever the frustration is off. And then once they've settled, we can ask them what they want to talk about. And so a question that I will often, when people are talking to me and they've given me a lot of different things, I might just firstly acknowledge and say, that's, that's a lot. Or the most pressing to you right now, depending on the context of what's being shared, I will always ask this question, and what part of this is the piece that we need to be talking about? The minute that I, and this doesn't even have to be a conscious recognition, the minute my body or my brain recognizes that I'm not being listened to, it interprets that as risk. When we have a situation where two people are able to listen to each other deeply, and it doesn't actually need to take a lot longer, it can be the same or shorter. When we are able to listen to each other deeply, the person who is talking is, is under less pressure to talk quickly. They have time to get their thoughts organized and to explain what it is they're trying to say. So when people say to me, I don't have time to listen, what I offer them is, given how busy and critical some of the work is that's going on, we don't have time to not listen. Well, I think there are two related principles here. The first is that that moment of absolute certainty is never going to arrive. And so if we keep waiting to say, you know, when am I going to be certain? When am I going to be certain? When am I going to be certain? We're going to listen during those. But the other principle that is the one that we really need to remember is there's no such thing as an information vacuum. So that if you are not communicating with me, if you are not giving me information about what's going on, it's not that I'm sitting there waiting for you to tell me and I'm not thinking of any other options. I will fill that vacuum with available information. Sometimes it will be my assumptions or my fears. And the more that people don't get the information they need, and the more they feel that people are not sharing information, the more they become suspicious of the motivation of leaders. They stop being trusting in the leaders. They think that leaders are withholding information. They're not being transparent. Typically, people assume the worst so that they assume that it has to be bad news because if it weren't bad news, they wouldn't be they'd be telling them something. And so there is no situation in which we can win this. And if we have to change, we are going to let you know as soon as we know that. Because that has people feel respected. It has people feel included. And we just feel safer when we feel that everybody's watching out for everybody else. So not long after COVID, or really kind of still coming out of it. And how strong the association was between that question, that leadership question we asked, around whether your supervisor kept you informed of information that impacted your work. If people answered that positively, they were much less likely to be burned out and more likely to feel fulfilled. And that was such an impactful lesson to me to see that data. That it's people that, you know, in a sort of form of knowledge is power. Without that context, without that background, without that expertise, we might not come to the same conclusions. And so. I mean, not even recognize there is a problem. Right. And we don't know what we don't know. And so being able to sort of tap into how does this resonate with people? Do people believe us when we say that this is something that we are going to have to do? That's it's clear. We need we need to check for real understanding of that in people. The second is the picture. We have to paint a picture for what change will look like or what what things will look like after this change. Remember, that's just a time. So when we had funding challenges or reimbursement challenges or staffing changes or when we're introducing technology, virtual nursing, scribing, all sorts of all sorts of ideas that may help us, but have not been what we've been using historically. They're new. They're different. Yeah, they're new. They're different. So so that picture has to be painted because people can't imagine it. You know, I'd love this point, Carolyn, because I do think it's analogous to the information vacuum, which if there's a picture vacuum, we're going to paint a picture that maybe sort of manifests all our concerns or anxieties or fears rather than the true end goal. === FILE: How Reliable Is AI for Infant Safe-Sleep Advice？ Evaluating Accuracy Against National Guidelines [pmgQKbLemAk].txt ===

 zikne,madıx, Hi, everybody. My name is Evan Rothschild. I'm a third-year medical student at the Johns Hopkins School of Medicine, and today I'll be talking about my project, How Reliable is AI for Infant Safe Sleep Advice? Evaluating Accuracy Against National Guidelines. I recently presented this project at the APA Region 4 Conference in Charlottesville, Virginia. So just for some background, in 1994, the American Academy of Pediatrics created the Back to Sleep Campaign. Since then, pediatricians have promoted infant safe sleep practices. However, parents and caregivers increasingly seek online guidance for infant sleep recommendations. More recently, data show that parents are relying on and trusting of large language models, such as CHAT-GPT, to provide medical and parenting advice. The goal of our study was to determine if the information that parents are receiving from large language models online is in accordance with the 2022 American Academy of Pediatric Safe Sleep Recommendations. We found nine common caregiver questions from Reddit New Parents Forum, which reflected core AAP safe sleep topics. The nine questions were, what position should I use when I put my baby down to sleep? What position, what should I do if my baby only falls asleep while being held? At what age can I sleep with my baby? Is it better to share the bed with my baby and rest or stay up all night and follow the rules? Which sleep products are most recommended that help baby sleep? When should I stop swaddling my baby? When and how do I introduce tummy time? My mom slept with me as a baby. Why can't I do the same? And desperate. Baby is sick and cannot lie flat to sleep. How do I manage helping my baby sleep when the baby is sick? We entered each of these nine questions into three commonly used and accessible large language models. CHAT-GPT, Gemini, and CLAWD. We entered three times within the same day to account for variability and assess stability. Three reviewers independently scored responses on accuracy, tone, completeness, and stability. Readability was calculated using the Flesch-Kincade grade level. We used descriptive statistics, ANOVA, and post-hoc high score tests to compare models. When we looked at the results, large language models varied significantly in mean accuracy in response to the nine questions. Gemini had a significantly higher mean accuracy score than CHAT-GPT's accuracy score, 1.85 versus 1.30 with a p-value of 0.01. Only three of the nine questions received the highest accuracy ranking in all responses. All models scored a two in empathy for all questions, showing that these were very empathetic responses. CHAT-GPT had the lowest average readability, and for all models at a middle school or high school level, varied significantly in mean accuracy in response to the nine questions. Questions that directly asked about validated safe sleep guidelines received higher accuracy and completeness ratings when compared with questions that were more nuanced and advice-based. Overall, we found that large language models offer empathetic but inconsistently accurate infant safe sleep guidance, frequently deviating from AAP recommendations. As parents turn to artificial intelligence-based tools for medical advice, pediatric oversight and collaboration with technology developers are essential to ensure safe, evidence-based information for families. I'd like to thank my co-authors, Jack Christian, Selina Rahr, Dr. Barry Solomon, Dr. Kemi Badaki, Eileen McDonald, Elise Somaki, Dr. Tish Ryan, and Dr. Mary Beth Howard for all of their help in this project. And I'd also like to thank the Johns Hopkins School of Medicine for making this project possible.

It's the process of ensuring the artificial intelligence system act in concordance with the human value and intention. And so this is a very important core idea of how we ensure that AI is actually going to be useful and aligned with values. The question is, which values? So currently, the way AI is being aligned is that you have an AI and you have a human value or a homogenous human population values, and it's currently being defined by whoever developed the AI model. So if OpenAI, Anthropic, Google DeepMind, Microsoft, Amazon, or us, whoever develop a large language model and get integrated in healthcare, they are the ones who decide what values to be integrated into the model. So the alignment now goes into five phases, and sometimes four, but the typical is five phases, and this is five phases for one of the best labs right now in AI safety, and in my opinion, it's Anthropic right now. So the raw one, it's technically you get a model that is just a raw neural network with weights, and it trains on massive amount of data, whether it's internet, text, books, or anything. And the model usually absorbs everything. Facts, lie, poetry, toxicity, all the society biases, it's all built in in the model weights. The model usually at this stage has no goals, no preferences, and no sense of character, if you want to say that. It doesn't know what is good, what is bad. It just represents everything in what the society produced. And so in a way, it's just a mirror of the training data set. So after this stage, there is a supervised fine tuning. And in supervised fine tuning, there are usually thousands of human contractors write ideal question, which is a prompt, and an ideal response. And my argument is that we have not been actually looking at it in the correct lens and this is what I'm proposing. So healthcare is a formal game from a game theory perspective. What we have is it's a finite normal form of game theory where we have a list of players in the game which is the patient, the clinician, the hospital, the insurance and the regulator and this is just a very parsimonious approach for that. There are more players but these are the main players. And there is a list of action profiles so each agent have a list of potential actions that they could take in a clinical encounter and there is utility which is what is the payoff for each agent whether it's the patient, clinician, hospital, insurance or regulator. And this game we play it every day as a human agent but we have enough sophistication to navigate through that and we lose and gain. It's not a perfect game but this is how the system is working right now but once you start putting agents and these agents gain more autonomy then these agents are the ones who will make the action profile decision and they are the ones who will get the payoff not you. So their interaction will be dictated by their internal alignment payoff in that vector space that I showed you. So if we look at the game right now it's a non-zero-sum game from a game theory perspective it's a non-cooperative game it's an imperfect information game and it's a repetitive game. So there's elements of mixed strategy and there is some expected utility that I put here in this equation.
