She was awarded an early stage investigator K award to receive graduate training for clinician scientists where she studied here for getting a master's of science and applied health sciences in informatics in our program. And her research is now supported by the National Eye Institute and research to prevent blindness is focused on promoting health equity in diabetic retinopathy care using informatics tools. Dr. Kai has quickly become a prominent leader in our in the Odyssey community for observational health data science and informatics. Her work focuses on bridging the gap between eye clinical eye care and global data standards ensuring that vision related outcomes are accurately represented in large-scale observational research. She does this in two ways. She plays a pivotal role in the Odyssey eye care and vision research working group working to map ophthalmology specific EHR data to the OMOP common data mill. And she's also led two large Odyssey evidence network studies, which have been multicenter studies to investigate treatments for retinal diseases and glaucoma aiming to identify disparities in care and optimize patient outcomes across diverse populations. At Johns Hopkins, Dr. Kai balances her informatics research with a dedicated clinical practice. Her academic pursuits focus on social determinants of health and how social economic factors influence the progression of blinding eye diseases. Cindy, thank you so much for coming today and presenting for us. Great. Thanks, Paul. Thanks so much for that very kind introduction. It's really a pleasure for me to give this grand rounds talk. Let me just make sure my slides are advancing. Does that, is that working? It looks great. All right, perfect. So these are the objectives of the, of the talk. Yeah, so maybe this is a little bit of an unusual informatics grand rounds and that it's going to be informatics tools from the perspective of a clinician. So like Paul mentioned, I'm a clinician by training, I'm a practicing vitro mental surgeon here at Hopkins. And early on in my career, I got the applied health sciences informatics masters here at Hopkins. And all this talk will kind of be about the journey of how I discovered this field and I'm going to be a going to be a part of a vision vision. I'm going to be a vision vision. I'm going to be a vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision vision the end of the day, you know, everything that I do is for the purpose of improving outcomes for my patients. So as a retina specialist, we see patients with diabetes and monitor them for diabetic retinopathy. And what I was noticing in my clinical practice was that we'd see patients with diabetes, kind of follow them for a period of time, and then all of a sudden, they would disappear for years and have a lapse in care. And by the time they come back, they develop this end stage disease with, for example, a tractional retinal detachment. And as a surgeon, I can fix this tractional retinal detachment and put the retina back where it belongs. But the vision outcomes, even after repairing this detachment, is not as good as if we had just prevented the retinal detachment from happening in the first place. And we actually do have great treatments to prevent this end stage complication. So a couple of insights that I had were, you know, these lapses in care, especially for diabetic patients, really are missed opportunities for early intervention. And when you talk to patients, you know, everyone cares about their eyesight, everyone is scared about losing vision. And it's a lot of times these other social factors that are preventing them from returning for care. So people talk about the fact that they can't find transportation to bring them to the clinic, or they have caregiver responsibilities for young children or their older parents or things like that, that prevent them from seeking eye care. And what was also really interesting to me was that for as much information as I enter into the electronic health record as a clinician, the electronic health record really wasn't giving me information back. It wasn't telling me when my patients with diabetes were having these lapses in care. And it certainly wasn't telling me if patients were at high risk for having lapses in care. And so zooming out of just my clinic, like really diabetic retinopathy is a problem nationally and really internationally. So even among, you know, even in the US, it's still the number one cause of vision loss among the working age adults and affects millions of patients. And these estimates are only going up because the number of patients with diabetes is going up year after year. And the way I think about, you know, diabetic retinopathy, it's in two components. So as soon as patients with diabetes, particularly patients with type 2 diabetes are diagnosed, they're recommended to get an eye screening to see the staging of their retinopathy. And even after the screening, they're supposed to ideally get at least annual eye exams and annual follow-up for whatever they have. Because at the end of the day, you know, these patients have to go to the eye doctor because all the treatments that we have to prevent blindness and all that follow-up really needs to happen in the eye clinic. So getting patients with diabetes to the clinic is really a fundamental problem. And so my clinical experience really has informed how I would like to tackle this problem of reducing vision loss from diabetes. And so my research program, like Paul mentioned earlier, is really focused around, you know, assessing and addressing social determinants of health because we know that's really important, you know, both on the individual level as well as the neighborhood level. And we'll talk a little bit more about that in a little bit. And really leveraging of our electronic health record, figuring out how we can use it to identify patients who are at high risk for lapses in care and vision loss from diabetes. And because, you know, vision loss from diabetes is such a national and international problem. What I would really like to do is share those electronic health record prediction models with other institutions and other clinics, because all those clinics will benefit. So to start talking a little bit about, you know, social determinants of health, they're the conditions in which people live, work, play, and the wider set of social structures and economic systems that shape the conditions of daily living. And they really play critical roles in health behaviors and health outcomes, either in a positive or a negative way. And they can affect patients on the individual level, or they can affect groups of patients and what we what I call kind of the neighborhood level. And you obviously social determinants of health are important from a health equity perspective. And you might be thinking from an informatics perspective, like, why are we even talking about this? But I think because they're so important in health outcomes and behaviors, that's one of the important reasons to think about this as we're, you know, thinking through informatics solutions. And so we've shown that social determinants of health are important for a wide range of diabetic retinopathy health behaviors and vision outcomes as well. In this particular study, we were looking at individual level social determinants of health and kind of annual eye exams on the national level. So we use pool data from the National Health Interview Survey of adults with diabetes. And the NHIS is a cool survey because it's a cross-sectional household interview run by the CDC. And because the the multi-stage area probability sampling allows for national representativeness. And so we leveraged 55 questions from the from NHIS to represent these various domains of social determinants of health following the Kaiser Family Foundation, ranging from economic stability, neighborhood and physical environment, education, food, community and social context, and the healthcare system. And our outcome was this question that asked patients whether or not they had seen an eye care provider in the preceding 12 months. So we're calling this self-reported eye care utilization. And so examples of adverse SDOH across each of these domains included, for example, an adverse SDOH in the economic stability domain was if the patients reported difficulty paying medical bills or having a low family income. Adverse SDOH in the neighborhood and physical environment was renting as opposed to owning a home. And in the education domain was having less than high school education or poor English language proficiency. So food insecurity, psychological distress, or being uninsured or not having a usual source of care. So we identified over 20,000 adults with diabetes in this pooled in his survey, but which represented a weighted sample of over 27 million patients. And 43% of them reported not seeing an eye care specialist in the preceding 12 months, which is roughly what other studies estimate as well. And what we found was that at having an adverse SDOH across each of the Kaiser Family Foundation domains was associated with lower odds of eye care utilization in the past 12 months. And we also looked at SDOH in aggregate and found that having just an increased overall burden of adverse SDOH was associated with lower odds of eye care utilization. And again, it was statistically significant in terms of a trend. So the N-HIS survey is obviously very powerful because it allows for national representativeness. But as you might have been able to tell, some of the questions were a little bit broad and not necessarily specific for eye care. And there weren't as many details in the SDOH survey as we would have liked. And so we've done other studies. This particular one is a little bit closer to home. It's using data from the Washington, D.C. Medicaid beneficiaries. This was a post hoc analysis of a prospectively collected cohort. And this cohort underwent a very comprehensive SDOH screening. And we found for this study, not having a regular doctor for health needs or having poor housing conditions was associated with not getting an eye exam in the two-year post-study enrollment period. So because we're asking more detailed questions, we're starting to get a sense of what potential targets we could intervene on to change, you know, to improve eye care utilization among patients with diabetes. But at the end of the day, what I want to create is an intervention for our Hopkins population. So because that's the case, it's still really important to ask patients that we ultimately want to intervene on. So this was a prospective survey of patients with diabetes who get eye care at Hopkins. And what we're showing here is a heat map of the association between the various SDOH variables that we included in this instrument on the log odds scale. And what you can see here, perhaps not surprisingly, is that many of these variables are correlated. So not having, you know, the highest degree earned was very correlated with the highest level of school, not surprisingly. And time without insurance was very correlated with not having insurance. And so it makes analyzing SDOH variables a little bit tricky. So in this case, we use a statistical technique called latent class analysis, which is a statistical clustering procedure to identify different social risk groups. And we found that patients generally fell into one of three groups. The first group were patients who had the least burden of adverse SDOH. They generally had financial security. They did have insurance. Their only adverse SDOH really was not having perhaps knowledge about diabetic retinopathy. Group two looked a little bit different. They started to have financial insecurity and difficulty with transportation, but this group did have insurance. And this is in contrast with group three, who had the most adverse SDOH. They had financial insecurity, often didn't have a high school degree, and this group also did not have insurance. I think this study emphasizes to me what's well known in the health equity literature, that in order to really effectively intervene on adverse SDOH, you need multidimensional interventions. Because at least in the ophthalmology space, a lot of people talk about improving education for diabetic retinopathy. But as you can see here, I think that type of intervention could work for patients who fell into group one, but perhaps wouldn't work for patients who are in group two or three. So really just keeping in mind the necessary, the need for multidimensional interventions of adverse SDOH. And not surprisingly, these different social risk groups were associated with vision outcomes. So patients in groups two and three were much more likely to have a history of lapses in their diabetic retinopathy care, and more often had vision that was worse than 2040, or having vision impairment. So social determinants of health can also affect groups of patients, what we consider what we call neighborhood level SDOH. And there are a whole bunch of different variables that describe neighborhood level SDOH. Our group has tended to use one that's called the Area Deprivation Index, which is a factor variable based on all of these different census data that you can see listed here. And a higher ADI indicates more socioeconomic deprivation. And we know this is important because areas of higher socioeconomic deprivation with concentrated poverty often lack public transportation and lack healthy access to healthy foods. So you can immediately see how that can affect health outcomes and health behaviors. And so we have shown the importance of neighborhood level SDOH for a variety of these diabetic retinopathy outcomes as well. In this particular study, we showed that patients from more from neighborhoods with more socioeconomic deprivation had a greater predictive probability of having lapses in care. And in this study that was done at two different academic institutions, again, patients coming from neighborhoods with more socioeconomic deprivation had a higher predictive probability for having proliferative diabetic retinopathy, which is the more severe form of diabetic retinopathy compared to non-proliferative diabetic retinopathy. So we'll come back to the SDOH idea a little bit later on in the talk. And I wanted to switch gears a little bit to talking about data. I think the topic that this audience is super excited about, and specifically big data, where there's a whole lot of volume, variety, velocity, and variability. And like I mentioned earlier, one of the challenges that I was seeing as a clinician was that patients would come in for diabetic retinopathy care and would have a lapse in that care and not return for a period of time. And what I was realizing was that actually being able to even define a lapse in diabetic retinopathy care in the electronic health record is a big data problem. And that's because, you know, ophthalmology is really a multi, it's multi-specialty. You know, sometimes our patients with diabetes will come in for their diabetic retinopathy screening or treatment, as you can see in these, you know, vertical red bars. But sometimes they come in for their other ophthalmic problems, like glaucoma or cataracts, as indicated by these blue bars. And from the data and from, because the fact that I, you know, did my training here, I know that these patients with diabetes are not really getting their retinopathy checked at these other visits. And so in order to define a lapse in diabetic retinopathy care, we had to do one of two things. First, we had to identify and isolate all the office visits that were only about their, that, you know, talked about their diabetic retinopathy screening or treatment. And we couldn't rely just on structured data in the form of ICD codes, because we were missing about half of those office visits. So we really had to turn to natural language processing of the free, the provider free text notes in order to isolate all the visits relevant for their diabetic retinopathy. And then the second thing we had to do was figure out when the provider even wanted the patient to return. So again, some of that information is in the structured checkout portion of the electronic health record, but a lot of it was buried in the provider free text notes. And once you have these two pieces of information, when the provider wants the patient to come back and when the provider, when the patient actually returned, and you can create these various thresholds. And we created this based on the literature. And so, you know, this patient, this provider wanted the patient to come back in four weeks, this person didn't do so until 12. And that, you know, was beyond the pre-specified threshold of 10 weeks. So this was considered a lapse in diabetic retinopathy care. So when we applied this definition to our patients with diabetes who come here for at Hopkins, we were finding that over a quarter of patients were having a lapse in diabetic retinopathy care. So we can do better for our patients. And it differed by race and ethnicity. We found that patients who were non-Hispanic, Black, Hispanic, or other had a much higher odds of having a lapse in diabetic retinopathy care compared to otherwise similar non-Hispanic white patients. And we know from the literature that having a lapse in care is associated with worse vision outcomes. And that's particularly true for patients who have complications from their retinopathy, for example, diabetic macular edema or the proliferative diabetic retinopathy who are undergoing active treatment. And similarly, in our population, having a lapse in care was associated with having vision impairment compared to patients who, you know, did not have a lapse in care. And this was true across patients of all different subgroups and diabetic retinopathy severity and treatment statuses. So now that we can define lapse in care, another really useful thing for us to be able to do is predict who is likely to have a lapse in care. So we've used random force models to see if we can predict lapses in care. And we used electronic health record variables such as, you know, socio-demographic characteristics, ophthalmic characteristics, and wait times and lead times. And we also tested models where we additionally added, you know, whether or not the patient had a history of lapses in care, and also added neighborhood level SDOH variables, like for example, the ADI that we had talked about earlier. And what we found was basically, yes, the area under the curve was close to 0.78. So we are able to predict who is likely to have a lapse in care. So this is important information. And so because being able to predict who's going to have a lapse in care is useful, you know, you can imagine a world in which for those particular high risk patients, you can offer them additional social resources or, you know, opportunities to bring them back in for care and reduce these lapses in care. And because vision loss from diabetes is really a national, international problem, we would like to be able to, you know, disseminate these predictive algorithms to other institutions that benefit their patients as well. But there are many data challenges in terms of disseminating these predictive algorithms. As this crowd knows, every the data, you know, data and various data sources can look very different. I think this is an example that Paul used in one of his intro to PMAP classes. But take example, take for example, the variable sex, it can be coded as male and female in one data source, and just M and F and another data source, or even with numbers in the third data source. So if your predictive algorithm uses sex as a variable, if you want to change it for data source two, and number three, you have to update that line of code. Now imagine doing that for the 10,000 variables that you're using in your predictive model, and trying to do that for 100 different data sources, that it becomes a problem that's just not scalable. And so the solution to this is to get all of your data to look the same, so that whatever algorithm you develop at data source number one can be easily deployable to the other data sources. And so that's where a common data model comes in, it organizes and standardizes data by imposing a common structure and vocabulary. And one of the commonly used one these days is the OMOP Common Data Model, or the Observational Medical Outcomes Partnership Common Data Model, and that's managed by the Odyssey community, the observational health, data sciences, and informatics community. So the Odyssey community really is a global community, it's kind of unified in this vision to improve health through observational research. And at its core, it's a global federated data network. I think the last estimates suggest that there are close to about a billion unique records in this global network. So it's a very powerful community and data network. And so I just wanted to share, you know, two network studies that, you know, Paul kind of mentioned earlier, to demonstrate how big data can be useful for ophthalmology research. And when I give this talk to the ophthalmology audience, I also, you know, promote the Odyssey approach and suggest that their way of doing observational health studies really should be more widely adopted across ophthalmology. So one of the first projects that we did was to look at the risk of kidney failure among patients who are getting intravitreal anti-VEGF medications. So we know that anti-vascular endothelial growth factor medications, when given systemically, have known adverse kidney side effects, including acute kidney injury and proteinuria, which are major risk factors for kidney failure. And we give these medications very commonly to treat a number of eye diseases, including diabetic retinopathy and all the related complications. And when we give these medications inside the eye, there is some systemic absorption. It's probably the greatest with bevacizumab and Aflabirceps, and it's the least with ranibizumab. So this has led some practicing ophthalmologists and retina specialists to preferentially suggest the use of ranibizumab for patients who are at high risk for kidney failure, for example, are patients with diabetes. And so that was the question that we asked in the Odyssey community. Is there any evidence to that practice pattern? Is the risk of kidney failure really different among patients who are getting ranibizumab, Aflabirceps, and Bevacizumab? And because of all the amazing infrastructure that's in place between the data model and all the open source tooling, we were able to ask this question across 12 different, very diverse databases in a matter of nine weeks. And you can see here, our administrative claims databases span from commercial claims to Medicaid claims, even claims from Japan, as well as included aggregated EHR data, Optum, VA, and individual EHRs like Hopkins and Stanford. We identified over 240,000 new users of monthly intravitual anti-VEGF medications, and found that in pairwise comparison, we really are not seeing a difference in the risk of kidney failure among patients who are receiving these three anti-VEGF medications. So this study answered a really important clinical question that, you know, we have as retina specialists treating these patients. And so for me, I no longer have to recommend the use of ranibizumab for patients that I'm worried are a higher risk for kidney failure. So one of the second network studies that we did in ophthalmology that was useful was looking at the association between semaglutide and non-arteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy or NAION, which is a blinding eye disease. So semaglutide is a GLP-1 receptor agonist that has many benefits in reducing cardiovascular and kidney complications, and it's recommended by the American Diabetes Association as one of the preferred treatments of patients with type 2 diabetes. And so July of 2024, a paper came out in JAMA ophthalmology that suggested patients who were on semaglutide had a four-fold increased risk for developing this potential irreversible blinding eye disease, NAION, compared to patients who were on non-GLP-1 receptor agonists. And they found that this was quite a common complication. The cumulative incidence of NAION among the semaglutide cohort was over 6%. There were limitations to this study. It was done at a single academic center that was a major referral center for NAION, and even the authors, you know, really said that in order to confirm, refute, or refine their findings, there need to be other studies, essentially. But the lay press kind of went a little bit wild with this information. And there were all of these papers that came out right afterwards, you know, warning people that this popular weight loss drug, semaglutide, is linked to irreversible vision loss. And it got to the point where our governing bodies of the American Academy of Ophthalmology and the North American Neuroophthalmology Society had to issue a public statement, again, reminding patients that the study was done at just a single large eye hospital that was a major referral center, and that at this time, they were not recommending patients stop taking semaglutide. And since that paper came out, there was a whole host of other observational studies that came out in the literature. And not surprisingly, we were seeing just a lot of different conflicting results. There were some studies that suggested there did seem to be a higher increased risk, and others that said, no, there is no difference in risk of semaglutide to other medications. And of course, it's really hard to compare between all these different observational studies, because there are different data sources. They often use different study designs or cohort definitions, and certainly analytic approaches as well. And so the Odyssey, for any Odyssey network study, it addresses a lot of these challenges, you know, for any network study that's done in Odyssey, there has to be a pre-specified protocol. So you can't p-hack, you can't, you know, invent comparisons later on, it has to be written before you even conduct the study. And all the phenotypes that are created are computable. So any other researcher who has access to OMAL common data, data can easily just rerun your phenotype. And no one has to guess what your phenotype looks like computationally based on the words that you're writing on the page. And all the tools that are developed are all open source. And in a single study, we're able to test different databases, outcomes, study designs, and sensitivities. So really improving the rigor and the reliability of network studies. And so we asked the same question in the Odyssey network. Does exposure to semaglutide confer a different risk of NAION compared to other GLP-1 receptor agonists or non-GLP-1 receptor agonist type 2 diabetes medications? And we tested this in multiple databases, looked at multiple different outcomes and study designs. So we included adults with type 2 diabetes who were exposed to semaglutide, dualaglutide, and exanatide, which were the GLP-1 receptor agonists, and the pagliflozin, citagliptin, and glupazide that were the non-GLP-1 receptor agonists. And we created two different phenotypes for NAION because there is no ICD code for NAION. So you kind of have to use these other rules and definitions to create a more reliable phenotype. And we looked at two different study designs, an active comparator, new user cohort analysis, as well as a self-controlled case series analysis. So in the active comparator, new user cohort analysis, we looked at, you know, patients with type 2 diabetes who were new users of semaglutide and on it as second line treatment for their type 2 diabetes after metformin. And we compared them to propensity score match new users of these other medications. We used large-scale propensity score matching and Cox proportional hazards models to estimate the risk. And for the self-controlled case series analysis, we took all patients with NAION and estimated the incidence rate ratio for NAION during semaglutide exposure compared to non-exposure using Poisson regression. And we did that similarly for each of these other medications. And as with other Odyssey Network studies, there was a whole set of study diagnostics for both analyses. And only the databases that passed all study diagnostics were included in the Bayesian random effects meta-analysis. And so for this study, we included 14 different databases. Again, a very diverse set of administrative claims databases and electronic health records, as you can see here, similar to the anti-VEGF study mentioned earlier. And for this study, we were able to get results in just four and a half weeks. Again, because of the tremendous infrastructure that exists in the Odyssey community. We found an incidence rate of NAION that was much smaller than the previous study. It was on the order of 100,000 person years as opposed to 100 person years in the prior study. And these estimates were much closer to what had been historically reported in the literature for, you know, incidents of NAION. And in the active comparator new user cohort analysis, we did not see a difference in risk of NAION comparing semaglutide to the other GLP-1 receptor agonists or the non-GLP-1 receptor agonists. And that was consistent, you know, the similar across either definition of NAION or outcome. The self-controlled case series analysis told a different story. So in looking at semaglutide, we found that there did appear to be an increased rate of NAION comparing semaglutide exposure to non-exposure. And that was consistent across both definitions of NAION. And we did not see an increased risk of NAION when we looked at the self-controlled case series analysis for the other GLP-1 receptor agonists, dual-glutide or exenatide, nor any of the non-GLP-1 receptor agonists listed here. So this study led us to also conclude that there does seem to be a small increased risk of NAION among patients with type 2 diabetes exposed to semaglutide, but it is much smaller than previously reported, probably on the order of 1.3, 1.5 as compared to 4. And the incidence rate of NAION is also much smaller than previously reported. Again, on the order of 100,000 person years, as opposed to 100,000 person years. And so our work and others led the EMA to convene a safety committee to review all the data. And they did conclude that NAION does appear to be a very rare side effect of medications with semaglutide. And they recommended that these medications update their basically black box warning. But again, you know, AAO and the NANOS have helped interpret these findings for clinical care, and they currently do not support a blanket recommendation for all patients to immediately stop taking semaglutide if they develop NAION. It really needs to be an individualized conversation between the provider and the patient, and it kind of depends on the patient's risk tolerance and things like that. So hopefully that demonstrates, you know, the Odyssey community is really powerful. It can answer some really clinically relevant and meaningful and timely questions and help influence care. But one of the things that we were noticing is that there are only a certain limited number of types of studies that can really be done in the Odyssey community right now, because there are still a lot of, we need to build capacity to do more vision research in the Odyssey community. So this was one of the earlier projects that we had done where we did a gap analysis to look at what's missing in OMOP essentially for vision research. So we took Kaleidoscope. This is basically the representation of the ophthalmic exam in EPIC. And we tried to match each of these fields and values to standard concepts in the OMOP vocabulary. And so the way we did it was we had a postdoc do the primary mapping. And there were two of us who had clinical training, clinical ophthalmology training, as well as informatics training, review the mappings and grade the mappings, basically answering this question of how well did the OMOP concept represent our source EHR data element. And we used the HL7 FHIR equivalent map equivalents to describe this relationship. So this is an example here. So in the EHR, there's a field called tonometry of the right eye, which can be mapped to intraocular pressure of the right eye. And that is an equal mapping. But there were other concepts like visual acuity of the right eye measured at distance with correction that we could only map to something like corrected visual acuity. And this is considered a wider mapping because this OMOP standard concept is missing things like the fact that this is in the right eye and it was measured at distance, which are two really important details that give us meaning for this visual acuity measure. In this example, the upper hortiolum of the right eyelid got mapped to internal hortiolum, which is a narrower equivalence because it's introducing this in this new concept of internal hortiolum as opposed to external. And there were some concepts that were just not unmatched. We couldn't find anything that was even remotely close to describing what we needed here. And so when we looked at the EPIC kaleidoscope, only about a quarter of concepts could be mapped equally into OMOP. A lot of them were wider, either missing laterality concepts or other concepts, and a lot of them were unmatched. So which really suggests to us more work needs to be done to build out capacity within Odyssey to do vision related research. And so we've kind of taken two different approaches to this problem of building capacity. So we know that OMOP draws from standard terminology like SNOMED and LOINC. So we have been working with those agencies, organizations to add terms that we need. And so in this project here, SNOMED had formed an eye care clinical reference group. And one of the first projects they did was to focus on glaucoma examination concept representation. And they did a project that was similar to the one that I described earlier, where they took all the exam elements relevant for glaucoma that was represented in EPIC and Metasoft and tried to map it to SNOMED and again labeled it with this HL7 concept map equivalence. And for all the ones that they couldn't map equally, they surveyed 10 different glaucoma specialists from multiple institutions to ask them, what is the significance of this gap? And do we basically do we need to add terminology into SNOMED? And at the end result of this project was that they added these various terms into the SNOMED CT and they got buy-in from the American Academy of Ophthalmology Glaucoma Committee as well as American Glaucoma Society to really endorse and say these are really critical concepts that we need to do better glaucoma research. And so these new terms got added into the SNOMED International and it went live in July 2023, which is really exciting. So we've been working with SNOMED and LOINC to add additional concepts. And the other approach that we've taken is to kind of focus on one extractable value. Because for things like visual acuity, there's about a billion different visual acuities in the EHR. For the purposes of doing, you know, large-scale research, you probably don't need every single one of them. You kind of just need one good visual acuity to represent, you know, the outcome. And so what is visual acuity? You cannot go to the eye doctor without getting your visual acuity measured. It is part of the comprehensive ophthalmic exam. It determines visual clarity and sharpness of vision. And it's basically testing the ability of the eye to distinguish between different optotypes, which are these letters and symbols done at a standard distance. And the whole goal of my field is to improve vision. And visual acuity is the endpoint of all clinical trials trying to answer this question of is drug A better than drug B at treating whatever disease? The endpoint is visual acuity. But visual acuity is really complicated. There is no standard ontology to describe how it's collected and all the complexities of it. So to give you a sense of the complexity, you know, the result of it can be recorded in Snellen ratio. It could be recorded in ETRS letters or Logmar or a Yeager or even a decimal. At least it depends which eye you're testing, either the right or the left eye or both eyes simultaneously. It depends on the chart that you're using, whether there have been additions to the chart like crowding, how far you're placing the chart from the eye. It depends what, you know, what you're putting in front of the eye. Are patients wearing their habitual correction? Are you doing a refraction? Are you using a pinhole? And of course, the state of the patient. And so, you know, like I mentioned, there's no, there's probably no need to represent every single different type of visual acuity in OMAP for us to do research. Maybe the thing to do would be just to look at all available possible visual acuities for a given patient at a given encounter for a given eye and extracts the best recorded one and use that for research. And so, this has been work that's, you know, ongoing since January 2025 to try to come to agreement for identifying what is that singular visual acuity value in the EHR that's most useful for ophthalmic research. And I'm happy to say that after a year of debating, we have finally come to a consensus and we are working on incorporating our ETL rules into the Themis conventions. And we're hoping, and we have one of our work group members work on creating the script for Epic and we're hoping to deploy it hopefully soon. So, because, you know, I did my master's here at Biz and I am giving the Biz grand rounds, I couldn't possibly give this talk without reference to Harold's informatics stack. The Harold here goes. I think what strikes me as I keep doing this work in, you know, using, trying to generate large scale evidence to improve vision outcomes is how much work has to be done both below the line and above the line. I think maybe personality wise, I love thinking about data standards. I love getting into the weeds of what an ETL script would look like or what the rules are. But at the end of the day, if you don't get buy-in from your stakeholders, like in this case, we needed our eye care vision research work group members to agree, or, you know, in the glaucoma example, you really needed buy-in from the American Glaucoma Society. So you need buy-in from individuals above the line to really make these solutions, you know, effective. So I think this is my, you know, one of my teaching points for our informatics students. Like maybe you'd be more comfortable working below the line and looking at data standards or algorithms or whatever, but just keep in mind that in order for your work to be really effective, you have to be thinking above the line, or in the very least, you have to partner with someone who's really good at above the line work. So trying to, you know, tie all of this stuff together. So how do we, how do these ideas come together? How do we integrate SDOH with the big data work that we're doing to really improve outcomes for our patients? Because that's what I ultimately want to do. So by increasing capacity of the Odyssey community to do vision re related research, there, there's a world in which we can build our prediction algorithms for predicting which patients are going to have in their diet care, or who's going to have poor vision outcomes on the OMOP data model, so that I can now disseminate it across all the data partners who are in this global Odyssey community. So that's the vision, that's why we're trying to expand capacity and do this work in Odyssey. And of course, you know, at the end of the day, we have to go back to the patients, because this we're trying to do this for our patients. So we've gone back to the patients to ask them, you know, is this an important thing for you? And like I mentioned earlier, unanimously, people want their vision to be improved, they don't want to lose their vision. We've asked them if it's okay, if they're comfortable, you know, talking and addressing social determinants of health issues in the context of their eye visit, and most patients are perfectly comfortable with that. And so I think a potential solution could be an EHR integrated tool that predicts when patients will have a lapse in care, and connecting those high risk patients with, for example, a community health worker, who are trusted members of the community in which they serve, who have a lot of knowledge about community resources, and can work as a liaison between community members and healthcare. And so we've launched a pilot study in at Hopkins for our patients at Wilmer, and most patients are very enthusiastic about working with a community health worker. And we're just analyzing data from our phase two of the pilot. And so the vision, you know, for the future, and what I'm kind of building towards, is a world in which, you know, when a patient with diabetes walks into my clinic to get their eyes checked, in the background, there's a prediction model that's working, that's, you know, using their socio demographic EHR data, their neighborhood level SCOH data, maybe put in some individual SCOH data in there, and will tell me if this patient is at high risk for not coming back for their diabetic retinopathy follow-up, or who are at very high risk for having vision loss. And I have this information at the point of care, so that I can refer them, for example, to a community health worker, who could do a social needs assessment of these high risk patients and get them linked to resources, so that our patients are coming back for care, they do not have a lapse in their care, I can institute interventions early on, as soon as they become, you know, at risk, to prevent these horrible late stage complications, like retinal detachments, and ultimately improve vision for our patients with diabetes. So I know we covered a lot of ground, from social determinants of health ideas, to health equity, to data, to informatics, because I think the most effective solution for this very real clinical problem of vision loss from diabetes probably lies at the intersection of all of these different disciplines. So hopefully this has been a useful talk to give, you know, ideas for our medical students or clinical trainees, the potential research direction and the clinical disease that you are interested in. And as it's a service as a reminder for our informatics students as well, to really find that clinical partner to work with, because they can tell you where the real problems are, so that you can start developing real solutions to help real patients. It is not a guaranteed price and your final charges may be higher or lower based on the services you receive. Second, the estimate includes hospital fees such as the cost of your room and supplies. Some estimates may also include physician or provider fees. However, not all estimates include these fees so this may not reflect your total cost. Third, by selecting yes I accept, you acknowledge that estimates are not contracts and that you are responsible for the actual charges related to your care including any difference between the estimated and final amount. Once you've reviewed the disclaimer, select the checkbox then click accept and continue to proceed. You can now search for a procedure by typing a keyword into the search field or you can browse the category section to find a specific service. In this example, we'll look up an estimate for an MRI scan with contrast. Enter MRI in the search field then click search. If you have received a procedure code from your doctor, you may enter it instead. The search results will display a list of related services. Select MRI brain with and without contrast. Next, you'll be asked to select your insurance. Choose your insurance provider or if you do not have insurance select continue without insurance. In this example, we'll select Blue Cross Standard PPO. Next, enter your deductible amount, co-insurance percentage and maximum out-of-pocket amount. If you do not have this information, select the I don't have option located beneath each field. Once you've entered the requested information, select use these benefits. Based on the information provided, we see an estimated cost of $505 in the you pay section. In the detail section, you'll see a breakdown of the total charges including hospital fees and what your insurance covers. You can print a copy of this estimate for your records by selecting the printer icon in the top right corner of the screen. Be sure to note the reference number when scheduling your appointment or calling with questions. You can also email yourself a copy of the estimate by entering your email address and selecting send email. If you would like to estimate the cost of another service, click select a different service and follow the same steps we just reviewed. We hope this overview of the patient cost estimate tool was helpful. If you have questions, please call our toll-free number at 844-986-1215. === FILE: Maie St. John, M.D., Ph.D., F.A.C.S. ｜ Head and Neck Surgeon [dtC9RAVCWCE].txt ===

 my road to becoming a head neck surgeon really began with my grandfather, who was a small town village physician. He would take me to see patients, and when I was about five or six years old, we saw a young woman who had a very large growth on her face, and I remember asking my grandfather, what is that? And he said, that is cancer. Of course, at that age, I had no idea what cancer was. What I did know is that I wanted to dedicate my life to helping patients with head neck cancer. My name is May St. John, and I am the professor and director of otolaryngology, head and neck surgery here at Johns Hopkins. I'm a head and neck cancer surgeon, and I see patients who have cancer, or growths could be benign growths also of the head and neck area, including cancers of the lips, the mouth, the tongue, the throat, the tonsils, the voice box, the thyroid gland, the salivary glands, and skin cancers. Much of the research that we do has really been brought forward by our patients. I had the privilege of caring for a young woman about eight years ago. She had a tongue cancer, and she and her husband had sought opinions all around the globe. When they came to my office, her husband asked me two very important questions. He said, Dr. St. John, number one, how much of my wife's tongue are you going to take, because we do have young children at home? The second question he asked me was, when you come out of surgery and tell me that everything went well, how will you in fact know that you have removed all of the cancer from my wife's body? I thought about those questions for the next few weeks, and as I was driving in to do her surgery, I realized that my car would beep when I was getting close to something of a different density. And I thought, as a cancer surgeon in the operating room, I am not only looking at the tumor, but I'm also palpating to ensure that I can feel the tumor. And this led to a very amazing collaboration with biomedical engineers across the globe, other surgeons and other patients, where we have built a device that allows us to see cancer cells in the operating room, to then be able to afford patients what we call precision surgery, removing all of the tissues that need to be removed, and preserving all of the tissues that allow them to maintain the quality of life. I've had the privilege of being a mentor in head and neck cancer surgery, and one of the things that I think is really important is it is a partnership with our patients. Patients bring to me an absolute perspective, an absolute joy, an absolute understanding, and allow us to have an empathy as we create a consensus of the care they need together. As I teach my trainees, I often tell them, if you take care of cancer, sometimes you win and sometimes you lose, but if you take care of the patient, you always win. Care of the patient may involve surgery, may not involve surgery, but what we are after, what we want to do is to understand the patient's priorities, to explain what the opportunities are, and to work together to ensure that we preserve and prioritize everything for that patient. Any patient that I have had the privilege of operating on becomes a member of my family. I know them, I know their names, I know their families, because they become a part of me. One of the things during cancer surgery is patients will come and we don't always know if we can remove the tumor in its entirety, but one of the moments I really treasure is when the tumor does come out in its entirety. I usually bend down and whisper in the patient's ear, although they're under anesthesia, the tumor's out, because it's really important to me and I want them to know, and so when my patients are asked, do you remember the moment that Dr. St. John mentioned to you that the tumor was completely removed? They always say, I know that she told me under anesthesia. Sometimes when you are handed a gift, you take a look and it may not have been what you wanted. Sometimes you're handed a gift and it's exactly what you wanted. Very few times in life I've been handed a gift where it has exceeded my every expectation. And that's what is true here at Hopkins. It is my privilege to be here, to care for patients with this community, and to be dedicated not just to being in the practice of medicine, but shaping the future of medicine for our patients.

I really especially liked acute care, children who are hospitalized, and then ultimately I really enjoyed working with children with medical complexity, especially when they're hospitalized. And that's a pretty common thing, especially in academic centers as a growing majority of patients are medically complex and need hospitalization. And I loved really running the gambit of seeing both infants all the way up to adolescents. I think they all presented with unique challenges. And I also loved a field that really prioritized medical education. I think a lot of the most salient times of learning as a resident came at the bedside and came during rounds. And so, I really wanted to pursue a field in which education wasn't just a thing that you did, but was really at the forefront of your practice. And so, I chose PHM as a career. What is your favorite role in Johns Hopkins? So I think something that's unique about Johns Hopkins is really its emphasis on both mentorship and sponsorship, especially in someone who is interested in scholarly work and in academics. And so, I think the emphasis on really learner-centric mentorship and sponsorship was something that I had already experienced at Johns Hopkins and something that I wanted to continue to have as I was emerging as someone who was interested in PHM and wanted to start a career in PHM. So really seeing me as a colleague, as someone who is going to be sharing within the field of PHM, I thought that was really incredible and something that I really cherished during fellowship. So I did a number of different projects during fellowship. One of my main projects in fellowship is really looking and exploring caregivers and their experience as they care for children with medical complexity, especially when they're hospitalized. I think there's been a lot of research in children with medical complexity or children with special health care needs, especially in caregiver burnout and their specific needs, mostly in the outpatient realm. But that question has not really been fully explored during an acute hospitalization. And so what I wanted to do was a mixed method study that looked at both the parents and their experiences during the hospitalization, but also to serve as a needs assessment. If we were going to co-design an intervention to really impact and improve caregivers experience as they were both either in their first time in the hospital or their 12th, their 20th time in the hospital with their child, how do we really improve their experience and their overall well-being? And so to really co-design it, I wanted to do this as a needs assessment to really understand their experiences and to try to create an intervention together that would benefit them. So I think one of the many things I learned during fellowship is it really takes a village to really set off a career in academia. What I learned is that although I did have experiences in research and in scholarship and residency and even in medical school, I realized that in order to really take the next step of a question that I had, I didn't have all the skills. I needed more mentorship, for example, in survey design and how do I really pursue a qualitative project? What kind of themes? What kind of methodology do I need to grow in? And so I really needed to find mentors in those specific expertise and create a coaching atmosphere in which I could really have a team to really push an intervention or a question forward. So I think that understanding that it really does take a village to pursue a specific question. I think that skill of both asking and understanding where are the deficits, where are the things where I need specific mentors or coaches in this area to really continue a question into the next phase and really continue a process until you really get to an intervention or a finding. And so I think that was really important during fellowship and I really received a lot of mentorship in the areas that I feel like I had a deficit in. And so I think having that mindset will continue to benefit me as I continue in my career. So I think that's a great question. I think personally for me, during fellowship, I had my first child and we were able to grow our family and of course I think part of wellness is, you know, having, being able to do the things in life that you want to do. And I think also an important thing about wellbeing, especially as someone who's in a trainee and someone who's early career is, do I get to pursue the sort of things in work that I think is really exciting? And I think one of the greatest things about the fellowship is really having a learner-centric model, which is instead of really, you know, giving you experiences and saying, hey, this is important for you to do as a fellow. Really the fellowship has the flexibility to really step back and ask, you know, what is, you've had this experience as a hospitalist, you know, in residency, you've learned certain skills. So how do, what are the things that get you excited? Because there's many things to pursue in PHM, whether it's QI, education, even more clinical expertise, whether that's POCUS, whether that's sedation. And they really step back and ask, you know, what's, what makes you excited and how do we give you the skills and the tools to really pursue that niche and to take that forward. And so to really have that experience, that flexibility and to really cater the curriculum to really develop in the skills that I thought were exciting. === FILE: Meet the Pediatric Hospital Medicine Fellowship Director [zDunjc6NKmc].txt ===

 alter bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear bear on any position anywhere in the country. Furthermore, our goal is to create people who are fantastic educators and who can do research and quality improvement like the back of their hand. People who are very familiar with all these things so that when they leave this program, they can find a job pretty much anywhere. The real principle here is we're trying to graduate people who can be leaders in the field of hospital pediatrics. To get there, one of our core values is really autonomy and guidance. We wanna give you the opportunity to spread your wings when you're ready to do so, and give you the opportunity to do and see the things you wanna see, but also provide you the guidance necessary for you to understand what you actually need to do in order to get where you wanna go. So, and also be aware of what kinds of jobs are out there and what you need to do. So it's a really comprehensive thing where we're trying to prepare you for a career in the field and also give you all the skills that's necessary to be an awesome leader in the field and a real impactful person throughout your career. The PHM faculty at Hopkins is a team, and we're gonna work together to get you where you need to go. We have expertise in a wide variety of things. We have researchers who conducted prospective randomized controlled trials. We have quality improvement people who are black belts in Six Sigma and provide you all the expertise you'll need to be an outstanding QI person. We have people who've done legislative advocacy, who've testified at the national house and Senate. We have people who have worked at the state level to get real foundational legislation through. We have people who have invented and patented devices and marketed them, created companies. We have all kinds of expertise. So we can bring this with us to train you in whatever you need to do. Additionally, what sets us apart is among the faculty are some real leaders. We have a previous chair of the section on hospital medicine, a first author on national guideline, a one of the leaders of the VIP network is here. So you're gonna have opportunity to work together with national leaders to really figure out where the field is going and how you can best be prepared, but also to give you the skills to become a national leader in the field. And those connections are super important in terms of building your career. Regarding the curriculum, I hope you'll look through the website and see what's out there and what our curriculum looks like, but it boils down to essentially a third, a third, a third. So a third of your time, you'll be on service on the hospital as service. And that will be both at our community site and in our main hospital. A third of the time you'll be doing research or QI, working towards your ultimate production of a paper, which you've either submitted or has been published in the field. And that's one of the graduation requirements. And then a third of the time is really up to you. And you can use that time to continue to work on your research or QI. You can use that time to do extra rotations in clinical stuff. You can learn POCUS, you can go abroad. You can pretty much do anything that fits within vaguely the field of hospital medicine. And I promise to be very sort of liberal about that as you think about your career, because I wanna have as much flexibility to help you go where you need to go. One of the key things during fellowship is to create lifelong relationships. And that occurs at national meetings where you'll bump into other fellows and other young faculty who are trying to do projects. And we're gonna hope that you start to work with them and bridge that gap. So you have a resource, other people in the field that you can talk to about projects that you can partner with at other institutions. Also, mentorships is really important. So I promise you, if you come here, you're gonna be stuck with me for the rest of your life, emailing you and seeing how things are going. But really all of us here are going to be really invested in your lifelong experience. You're creating a cohort of colleagues that you can go forward with in all of your endeavors. Wellness is critical in our careers and everything we do. And wellness is more than just having the time to go out with friends on a Saturday night. Wellness is also about enjoying your job. And a lot of your time with us is going to be spent on thinking about what makes you happy and how you can make sure that your career involves happiness. Let me give you an example. Right now you might be thinking to yourself, I don't really like research. And when you think about your research, it was an experience, let's say in college where you were just pipetting and didn't really understand why you were doing it, doing some sort of work in a laboratory. That's not what research is about. And that's certainly not what research in PHM is about. Research in PHM is about finding a problem and wondering how to fix it. Just today we were asking, well, if a prepotent is better for cannabis hyperemesis, has anybody studied this? Should we do a retrospective cohort study? If we figured out it was, we could be massively impactful in terms of shortening hospitalization for those kids who come in with hyperemesis. And that was just today's thought. So there's lots of opportunities to ask these kinds of questions and answer them so that we can really improve the lives of children. Flip side, if you're into QI, it may be that you know that steroids aren't indicated in bronchiolitis, but everyone's giving them anyway. How can you change culture in a hospital to get people to accommodate and do more evidence-based care? These are the kinds of skill sets that we want to engender. And by engendering those skill sets, by having fun answering questions, you start to create wellness by virtue of the fact that you're enjoying your work. The goal here is to maintain happiness in your career. And by doing and finding a way to work that into your career, it creates wellness in the face of a full-time job. And that's one of the big things we have to work on together. So one question you might be asking is, is Hopkins the right place for you? I don't know the answer to that question. What I can say is, we are looking for people who enjoy learning. We're looking for people who enjoy the process. We're looking for people who wanna have fun during fellowship and not just view it as a step in the right direction to some necessity that they haven't really considered there carefully. I would love to take people who have extreme experience in research or QI and beef things up. I would also like to take people who are excited to learn and have no experience and wanna start from scratch. And we will walk you through the process. We will hold your hand. There won't be any mysteries. We can get you to the point where you're comfortable with those things. We're also really interested in people who are thinking outside the box. Maybe you wanna do global health. Maybe you wanna do something else. We'll work with anyone who's interested in pursuing something. Because we have such an amazing varied team, we can accommodate learning in all kinds of areas. I hope that gives you a good general introduction. Look through the website, check things out. Please do not hesitate to contact us if you have any questions or concerns about the program. And hopefully we'll see you on the interview trail. Good luck in your journey.

=== FILE: Nakul S. Talathi, M.D. ｜ Pediatric Orthopaedic Surgeon [9j9xrD7agE8].txt ===

 playing a number of sports growing up, I spent my fair share of time in pediatric orthopedic surgeon's office. I remember fondly their ability to connect with me and their hard work to get me back onto the field. Now that I'm in their role, I feel honored to be able to serve the same purpose and work hard to get children back to doing what they love. My name is Nick Talladi and I'm a pediatric orthopedic surgeon at Johns Hopkins Medicine. I specialize in treating hip and spine conditions in children, young adults, and adolescents, along with orthopedic trauma and fracture care. In addition to clinical care, I'm interested in research to improve on how we make decisions for children with hip conditions, spine conditions, and fractures. In particular, I'm interested in optimizing everything we do to treat these children in order to provide the best outcome possible as well as to prevent any unnecessary surgeries. My philosophy for treating the child and their family is a listening first approach. I'm drawn into pediatric orthopedics because children are such a special patient population to treat. The decisions we make now, along with their families, can provide the child with decades of improvement and benefit. Additionally, I like to work with each family to tailor a specific plan for each child. One of the reasons I chose to practice at Hopkins Medicine was the opportunity to collaborate with leaders in the field in research, education, and clinical care. Ultimately, this will help us improve the care of each and every child we treat.
