 veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc veloc at the University of Colorado Health Science Center. He's really developed a leading research program in neuro-HIV where he uses innovative MEG-based, I can never say it, Tony, but MEG-based dynamic functional mapping. It's resulted in over 350 publications in journals and top journals, PNAS, Brain Biological Psychiatry, and he served as PI or MPI on well over a dozen NIH and NSF-funded R01 awards. He currently leads two NIH P20 centers, and he mentors multiple NIH-funded trainees. They've all been extremely successful, and his research really focuses on HIV-related inflammation and brain dysfunction, the impact of substance use in people with HIV, and comparisons between people without HIV and Alzheimer's-related cognitive impairment. So with that, I'm going to hand it over to you, Tony. I'm super excited to hear your talk, and then I'll kind of pop back on and help moderate at the end, okay? Awesome. Well, thank you for the very great introduction. The, uh, um, make sure my slides come through this time. Yes, awesome. Okay. All right. Are you seeing them? Yep. Great. Um, so, uh, uh, thank you for the very friendly introduction, and, uh, thanks for, uh, to everyone who was, um, involved in inviting me to speak today. The, uh, it's always great to, I think, talk at these, uh, NIMH, uh, um, HIV-AIDS centers. Um, I think they're really a great thing. It's a, uh, good way of leveraging, um, the resources at institutions. And, uh, from my own perspective, my first, um, uh, research funding as a faculty member actually came from one of these, uh, P30 centers. And it really had a huge impact on my career as far as, uh, just the funds, but then also, uh, the mentorship that I got through, um, the, that center as I was, uh, kind of launching my career, um, as a faculty member. So, uh, very excited to, uh, to speak today about some of our, um, the historic and also recent work in inflammation and, uh, cognitive impairment in people with HIV. Uh, so first off, uh, support and disclosure. So, uh, pretty simple from my perspective. Um, the, my laboratory, uh, is, um, funded by the NIH. Some of our, um, methods has been historically funded through the NSF. But for the most part, we're funded by NIDA, NIMH, and the Office of the Director in regards to some of our instrumentation. So the data I'm going to present today is mostly collected at the Institute for Human Neuroscience. So most of the tools we use here for neuro-HIV research, MRI, which I'll talk a little bit about at the end, some of the recent stuff we're doing. We also use PET imaging. There's a state-of-the-art PET scanner in Omaha that we share with another institution and use quite a bit. And then most of the work I'll present today is NEG, or Magnetoencephalyography, which is the instrumentation shown there to the right. So the Magnetoencephalyography has a lot of really cool advantages. It's not nearly as common as MRI, but it's totally non-invasive like standard research MRI. It's totally silent, which is, of course, a slight benefit over MRI. It has good spatial precision, usually about three to five millimeters for modern systems. The spatial precision does vary based on how people process the data. And then it has excellent temporal precision, so typically millisecond precision is attainable. A really unique feature is that it's an absolute measure of local electrical activity in populations of neurons. So unlike EEG and some of the other techniques, even most fMRI, that's a relative measure. MEG gives you absolute measures that you can directly interpret, which is really powerful in many contexts. It's also, as I mentioned, a pretty rare technique still. So I did a PubMed search last night, and it looks like there's still only about maybe 35 MEG studies published that are examining neuro-HIV. As far as the signal origins, pardon the 1980s figures here, but the physics doesn't change too much. So anytime you have electrical current, whether it be in light power lines or active neurons, you have a magnetic field that goes around that current in a very standard way following the right-hand rule. So the way MEG works is that you have populations of neurons. You have primary currents that are in the apical dendrites. Each of those neurons is creating a magnetic field when it has current flow. Those magnetic fields locally summate because magnetic fields are superimposable. And when a population of neurons is synchronously active and large enough, that generates a magnetic field that can be measured outside the head. And that's what we're doing in MEG. So let's see my pointer here. There we go. So over here, this is the chirogenic DUAR. So MEG uses liquid helium for cooling. You can see the person that has their head in this example array. So in our system, there's 306 magnetic sensors. They're being bathed in liquid helium. So they're superconducting temperatures, negative 450 degrees Fahrenheit. The liquid helium is just a few centimeters from the scalp during the actual recording, but there's really strong thermal shielding there. So it feels cool to the head, but not uncomfortable. And essentially what's the basis of the measurement, the neurons I spoke about, they're giving off magnetic fields. Those magnetic fields hit these superconducting sensors, and then that induces a current. And we're in the sensor, and we're quantifying that current at each of the 306 locations. So in regards to analysis, so some of the studies you might see, like, out in the literature, they actually stay in sensor space for MEG, which they're actually just kind of interpreting the magnetic fields they measure. There's nothing wrong with that, except that the spatial precision is not really what's possible through more advanced signal processing methods. So the technique we use is called beamforming. And basically what we do is we take the head, we divide it into voxels, so very similar to, like, a functional MRI scan. Our standard voxel size is 4x4x4 millimeters. That's user determined, so you could go finer, you could go higher. Depending on the cortical region, you can probably get beyond 4 millimeters in the paradigm. But 4 millimeters to us is just a good kind of middle of the road. The way this works is that you have a – we basically treat it where we have the 306 sensors. We're looking at, as a function of time, signal covariance across those sensors. And then we basically triangulate based on those patterns to estimate the amplitude of current that could have produced those signals per voxel across the whole brain volume. So what we're left with at the end of a recording is essentially a matrix of cubes or vertices if it's cortically constrained. And for each of those, you have a time series. The time series has the same temporal resolution as the original recording, so in our case, usually millisecond level. The way of understanding this time series is that it's a direct – excuse me, it's a complex signal. So there's lots of things intermixed in here, just kind of like if you were to have a radio that's, you know, without a tuner. So what we take is we do a time frequency decomposition of the signal and pull it into constituent signals. So like in this case, theta, alpha, beta, and gamma. And the way of thinking about this is that each of these are a different frequency of activity. So going back to the radio example, there's different neural events that are at different frequencies, just like there's different songs going on at different frequencies in a conventional radio. One of the reasons to decompose this is that because of the 1 over F rule, if you don't decompose it, you're basically only sensitive to the lowest frequency activity because it is going to be the strongest. So in neuro-HIV studies, the most common outcome measures are usually task-related neural oscillations. So these are really brain oscillations or changes in the signal power as a function of time related to some event in a cognitive experiment. So usually these are divided into delta, theta, and gamma frequencies. And during cognitive activity, you usually see increases in the power of those. That is time-locked to the cognitive event or stimulus event in sensory neuroscience. You also see alpha and beta responses. So these are usually power decreases. So when like a stimulus occurs, you can see in a specific brain area that alpha usually will decrease, particularly in the occipital cortex during visual paradigms and in the motor cortex for beta during motor paradigms. There's also spontaneous cortical activity. So spontaneous activity can be looked at a couple of different ways. So this is really just your seemingly random discharges and neuronal rhythms at rest. So you can have a resting state recording and estimate how much just spontaneous power there is in each of your voxels or vertices across the cortex. You can also look at the baseline and cognitive paradigm. So look at the time period that's occurring before the onset of the stimulus and see like whether the spontaneous activity during that period differs with aging or differs by group or things like that. You can also look at evoked responses, very similar to event related potentials or ERPs that are very commonly looked at in the EEG. Surprisingly, there's actually been very limited work in this area in neuro-HIV. I think it's just a lot of the people who were examining their HIV with MEG started looking at oscillations and that really caught on. And it's just kind of no one's really ever explored ERPs or also called event related fields sometimes in MEG. So kind of unpacking the differences between spontaneous activity and neural oscillations. So there was an influential study that was done about a decade ago by my graduate student at the time. And what this study was really looking at is the strength of beta responses during motor performance in older versus younger individuals. And what the study showed was that older individuals, the responses were about twice as strong. So you can see the maps here left and right. This is averaged across groups of about 30, I think, per condition or per age group, younger and older. So you can see the spatial distribution is very equivalent. And the images have been thresholded so that the size of the activation looks the same. But if you look at the scaling for the older individuals, it's about double. It is double of the younger. And if you look at the time frequency decomposition at the peak voxel, you can see it's much, much stronger right around the movement onset, which is time zero for the older individuals versus the younger. Now, if you pull out the time series of that peak voxel in each of the hemispheres, the left and the right, what you see is that the actual oscillatory amplitude, which is usually computed by normalizing the whole time series by the baseline in each person. So that's why you see here that it's like mean of zero prior to the movement. And then you have movement onset at zero and the sharp decrease in beta. So as you can see here, you see the big difference in older people versus younger who are shown in black, both in the left and the right precentral gyrus. But if you don't necessarily have to normalize the signal. So if you don't normalize it, it's called an absolute power time series. And then basically what you're just looking at is the overall signal power in that voxel as a function of time. And when you do that, you can see that the actual spontaneous beta in the baseline is much, much stronger in the older individuals versus the younger. And because of that, they have a much larger decrease when they go to move. And this decrease is the amplitude of the oscillation. It also correlates with lots of behavioral parameters. So the idea here is that basically spontaneous activity, the higher it is, particularly in alpha and beta, the worse it is for performance and the worse it is for as a proxy for brain health. The one thing about that, so this is looking at it as, you know, before an event. So since most of these experiments, a person is seeing stimuli like in a series. So, you know, let's say, you know, 50 trials or something like that. One could argue that these effects are due to the brain just not recovering as quickly from the stimulus. So to address that, we, and the same people looked at resting beta power. So this is just a person sitting in the scanner. We estimated the strength of beta activity in the same voxel across like a five-minute rest. And as you can see here, it's the same effect. So much, much stronger activity in older versus younger individuals. So really it's more just the nature of spontaneous activity versus how quickly it recovers. So one nice thing also about looking at spontaneous and oscillatory activities, now there's really a well-developed literature in regards to normative data. So this is a paper that one of my graduate students led a couple of years ago that was published in PNAS that looked at 430 individuals across the lifespan and the overall distribution of their spontaneous activity during rest. At the time, it was the largest study. Since then, there's been others that are larger. But essentially what it did was it divided the overall spectrum into canonical bands using what's the most common in the literature, and then computed maps for each of those, each of the canonical bands. That's what's shown here at the bottom. The relative map here is common in this literature, and it's an expression of how much power there is in each band relative to the total spectrum, whereas the absolute is the measure that's most commonly used in clinical neuroscience as the actual amplitude of spontaneous activity. But as you can see here, basically you have like theta is very strong in frontal cortex, whereas alpha is much stronger in occipital cortex, beta is stronger in motor cortex, and then gamma in prefrontal cortex. And delta, you typically see stronger in the frontal cortices, even though it's a little hard to distinguish here. So what this paper was looking at was just how the spontaneous activity varies across the lifespan. So here we're looking at the linear effects and where it peaks. So delta, the overall age effect from 6 to 84, which is peaking in the posterior left temporal. For theta, again, it's kind of a similar region. And then here's just your alpha. Notice that the actual overall pattern switches between theta and then alpha, beta, gamma. And just as you get older, there's essentially lower, well, actually the lower power in your lower frequencies and increases in power in the higher frequencies as you get older. And over here on the right is the actual absolute measures. So this is a relative on the left, absolute on the right, very similar. We also looked at the quadratic effects, so whether there was effects where it exceeded the quadratic explained significantly more variance than the linear effects. And so there we saw that that was true for delta, the sum theta depending on the region, the alpha, beta, and then gamma really kind of across the board. But the basic take-home here is just that the age-related effects on spontaneous activity are pretty well known. And the overall distribution in regards to strength that you would expect in different brain areas is also well mapped out. There's also been studies looking at the reliability of some of these indices. So Brandon Liu was one of my graduate students, now a resident at Brown in psychiatry. And some of the studies he did looked at how these maps vary across a three-year time period in controls. And so this is a map of just the distribution of alpha power. Again, you can see much stronger in the occipital, decreasing as you go forward. And then down here is a map of the ICC, interclass correlation, looking at just how consistent it is across the three years. And as you can see, it's pretty relatively high values. Each of these lines show an individual person. We did a later study, one of my research assistants in the lab who looked at whether this held for the task-related activity. And so here, this is a visual response. ICCs on the right axis, amplitude of the response is on the left, times on the X. And as you can see, the pretty robust reliability really across those time periods and even higher reliability for the lateral occipital alpha response, which is also really seen in almost every visual paradigm. So some of the early work that we did in neuro-HIV using these oscillatory and spontaneous metrics focused on visual attention. And so really the first paper I would say that was really had cool findings was published in BRAIN in 2018, led by a graduate student, Alex Weisman, at the time, who now directs his own lab in Vancouver. So this study involved three groups. I think it was a total of about 75 people split equally between the three groups. We had uninfected healthy controls, people with HIV who were unimpaired and then cognitively impaired persons with HIV. Each of the individuals did a visual spatial attention task during the MEG. So in this task, they're basically following this grid and responding in regards to its location on a screen. We look at reaction time and accuracy. So what we saw was really kind of a stair-step effect in reaction time in particular, where controls were the fastest and they were significantly faster than unimpaired people with HIV. And then both of those groups were significantly faster than cognitively impaired people with HIV. Accuracy-wise, the impaired group was significantly less accurate than both of the other groups who did not differ from each other. So we looked at the MEG signals. So as I mentioned, the first thing we do in all these analyses is really do a time-frequency decomposition to see where the actual responses are. And this uses a nonparametric permutation testing method since we're really doing tests on individual spectrograms. So what we found was that there was robust responses in theta, alpha, beta, and gamma. So we imaged those and then extracted the time series from the peak voxels bilaterally for each of those responses. And then we averaged them here just to show the basic time series of the theta response. And then next to that, the alpha response and then gamma down here in the bottom right. We also imaged the beta, but as you can see, this is really just a motor response. So we didn't spend too much time really looking at that. When we looked at the theta responses by group, what we found was really a stair-step effect that was very similar to the reaction time data, where we had strongest responses in controls, the marginally weaker responses in the unimpaired people with HIV, and then significantly weaker responses in the impaired group. Next, we looked at spontaneous activity in the same peak voxels. And so first we looked at the gamma frequency responses. And what we found here was that the spontaneous activity was much weaker in controls, so more of like your healthy response. And here, keep in mind that this is being computed before stimulus onset. So stimulus onset is at time zero. So this is basically the neural activity in these significant gamma response areas prior to the onset of the stimulus. So controls had the weakest, and then the people with HIV who were impaired and unimpaired had significantly elevated relative to controls, but did not differ from each other. When we looked at the peak alpha response, spontaneous-wise, again, this is the same time window, what we found was that spontaneous alpha activity was significantly elevated in the cognitively impaired people with HIV, and the unimpaired groups did not differ. Their spontaneous activity in regards to alpha was almost equal. In a related study, we looked at the selective attention function in three groups that had overlapping participant populations. So here in this paradigm, the task is basically that individuals focus on the fixation cross, and then a line of arrows appears. And the task is to respond left to right based on the direction of the arrow in the middle. And so there's both congruent and incongruent conditions. So in the incongruent condition, the flanking arrows are interfering, so they're pointing in the opposite direction. And in the congruent condition, all of the arrows are pointing in the same direction. So there's four possible trial types that are shown here. When we looked at the behavioral data, we found a significant flinker effect in all groups. So that's just that the interference is slowing down responses in each of the groups. So the task actually works is basically what that's saying. And then we also found that the controls were marginally faster than impaired people with HIV, and that both groups differed from the impaired group. When we look at the actual effect of interference, so what was the difference between the incongruent and the congruent condition? What we found was that the controls were the best at inhibiting the interference effect, whereas the unimpaired people with HIV were significantly worse, and then the impaired group was worse than both groups. So the first analysis we did was just looking at an ANOVA across both conditions. So we imaged the theta and the alpha responses, and then we conducted a voxel-wise whole brain ANOVA to pull out the significant peaks. === FILE: Produce Home Delivery, Social Resource Connections and Well-Child Visits in Pediatric Primary Care [S3ndXpVXO6k].txt ===

 higiest causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa causa Solomon, Professor of Pediatrics and Division Director of General Pediatrics. We are excited to tell you about a recent study that we presented at the annual Region 4 Academic Pediatric Association meeting in Charlottesville, Virginia. The title of our study was Impact of Produce Home Delivery on Social Resource Connections and Well-Child Visit Attendance in Pediatric Primary Care. Our full team includes Amy Lee, Annie Garrido, Naomi Mao, Emma Vail, Andrea Young, and Melissa Lutz. Our research explores the impact of a produce home delivery program for families who screen positive for food insecurity in pediatric primary care. Hopkins Community Connection is an innovative social needs program integrated into Johns Hopkins affiliated clinics to identify and address essential needs of patients through universal social needs screening and navigation to community resources and public benefit programs. For over 19 years, clinicians and staffs have referred families to HCC, which is grounded in building mutually beneficial relationships with community partners in our surrounding communities. HCC advocates, Johns Hopkins undergraduates, and community health workers work one-on-one with patients and caregivers to ensure successful resource connections are made. As such, HCC has allowed our clinics to take a holistic, family-centered approach to providing health care. Based on the needs identified in our clinics, HCC has developed innovative approaches to addressing food, financial, and transportation insecurity, social determinants of health, and systemic causes of inequality, inequity, and disease. Food insecurity is a household-level economic and social condition of limited or uncertain access to food, the root causes of which can be multifaceted and complex. In the United States, one out of seven children are affected by food insecurity. In Baltimore, the food insecurity rate for children is closer to one in three. We know these children face higher risks for chronic health conditions, more emergency department use, and fewer completed well-child visits. Families may face daily stressors which impact their ability to engage in preventive care consistently. Resource navigation programs like HCC could alleviate barriers, link families to resources, and facilitate access to care. In the Harriet Lane Clinic, families are routinely screened for food insecurity and linked to HCC when a goal is identified. For our study over a two-year period, we looked at two groups of families who screened positive for food insecurity. One group received both resource navigation support through HCC and monthly produce deliveries from a community partner for six months. The other group received HCC resource navigation only. We wanted to understand whether something as simple as delivering healthy food regularly to a family's home for a short period of time could help strengthen their engagement with HCC and their connection to other social resources and even improve their child's well-child visit attendance. We used data from the electronic health record and the resource navigation platform to look at the differences between the two groups for the proportion of non-food resource connections as well as well-child visit attendance over the subsequent 12 months. We were excited to find that those who received home produce delivery had a higher proportion of non-food related resources. Although we were unable to show a significant difference in the statistical analysis, it appears children three years and older may be more likely to attend all recommended well visits. What we found is that for families with food insecurity, in addition to HCC resource navigation, produce delivery may serve as an approach that builds trust, strengthens relationship, and reduces barriers to care. Families who received produce deliveries were more likely to connect with other non-food related resources and they showed a trend toward better well-child visit attendance for kids three years and older. This suggests that a seamless, low barrier connection to food resources can satisfy a family's basic needs, providing additional capacity to engage with other resources that may require more time and more follow-up. Our key takeaway is simple, yet powerful. An effective resource navigation program should aim to provide tangible resources that meet patients' most pressing goals while supporting access to broader community services and benefits. This can increase engagement, build trust, and help families stay connected to preventive care. Something as basic as fresh produce delivered to a family's doorstep can have a ripple effect and strengthen a family's connections to the healthcare system and thereby supporting better health outcomes. We hope this research adds to the existing literature and inspires continued investment in programs that meet families where they are and addresses social needs as a routine part of providing high-quality healthcare. We hope this morning everyone here is here. Thank you.

What I wanted to do today in the few minutes that I have is talk about the power of advocacy and the responsibility that we as providers have to advocate for our patients. Of course, academic medicine is centered on three pillars, clinical care, research and education. But I want to add a fourth pillar, advocacy. I want to begin with a simple truth. Advocacy is medicine. It may not come in a vial or show up on an MRI scan. But for patients living with chronic rare diseases like Heidi's advocacy can be as vital as any prescription. It is the engine that drives awareness, research and ultimately access to care. When we talk about chronic rare diseases, we're actually talking about the statistic that rare diseases impact over 25 million Americans. Not so rare. Behind every statistic lies a person who has spent years searching for answers, often through pain, uncertainty and invisibility. For them, advocacy is not abstract. It's survival. Many of these patients endure what's often called the diagnostic odyssey, as Rob detailed. Years of symptoms, referrals and mislabeling before someone finally names the disease. During this journey, advocacy begins, sometimes quietly, with a parent who refuses to accept we don't know, or a patient who keeps pushing for one more test to find the answer. Heidi and Rob are great examples of this. As clinicians and researchers, we must recognize that our roles extend beyond diagnosis and treatment. We are also translators, educators and sometimes the first advocates on a patient's behalf. Small acts, listening, documenting clearly, connecting a patient to a registry, can change the trajectory of a patient's life. As Francis Peabody, the great Harvard physician said, the secret of the care of the patient is in caring for the patient. So advocacy gains power when individual voices become collective ones. Organizations like NORD or the APPD Foundation have transformed research agendas and policy landscapes. The creation of the Orphan Drug Act, for example, emerged directly from patient advocacy, a testament to what can happen when determined individuals mobilize around a shared purpose. Advocacy for our patients also matters within institutions, especially complex and bureaucratic organizations like Hopkins, of course, not KKI, that change only slowly. When physicians champion early screening programs, establish multidisciplinary clinics or advocate for flexible trial designs as for Heidi. We can create change beyond our own patients. Every time we push a grant committee, an insurer or a regulatory body to see the person, not just the paperwork, we redefine what equitable care looks like. In rare disease research, the scientific excitement of a novel mutation or a pathophysiological mechanism should never eclipse the human face behind it. Advocacy bridges that gap. It reminds us that every data point in a natural history study corresponds to a lived experience. The parent tracking symptoms overnight, the patient participating in a study despite travel and cost. As physicians and researchers, we are in a unique position to amplify these narratives responsibly, to bring them to advisory boards, funding panels and classrooms. The science advances faster when it is fueled by empathy and grounded in the voices of those we serve. So what does this mean for us clinicians, scientists, caregivers? It means using our expertise and our credibility, not just within our disciplines, but also in public spaces where decisions are made. It means mentoring trainees to see advocacy as part of professionalism and not just a passion project. It means sharing power, inviting patients to co-design studies, to sit on panels and to critique the systems that affect them. Ultimately, advocacy transforms despair into direction. For someone living with a chronic rare condition, knowing that their story matters, to science, to medicine, to society can be profoundly healing. So let's remember that our voices, our data, our influence, all carry weight. When we align them in the service of those who too often feel invisible, we make the invisible visible. And that is the true power of advocacy. Patients don't care much how much you know until they know how much you care. So patient advocacy isn't optional or heroic. It's the ordinary work of making sure our patients get the care we'd want for ourselves. Thank you. Good morning. Good morning. It's a pleasure to be here. So I'm going to talk a little bit about chronic pain in the context of trying to help Heidi through that. So with all conditions in chronic pain, I can take away from patients a lot of different lessons. And the reality is that everything in the human body has an effect on everything else. And oftentimes we have this divide between medicine and psychiatry, but there's no division. That's an illusion to say that the brain and the body aren't connected. We see the world through the lens of our specialty. And in some ways it's beneficial that medicines become so specialized because we can treat these nano rare disorders. But at the same time, it creates these silos where patients with rare disorders oftentimes are just bouncing between the walls of different providers and rarely find a home base. Having a Dr. MacArthur as the quarterback is pretty rare. Having a Dr. MacArthur as the quarterback is very rare. And if you're treating patients, then it's very likely that you are seeing people with chronic pain. Because chronic pain affects one in five individuals in our country. And it is more prevalent than diabetes, heart disease, stroke and cancer combined. So it's important that we're thinking about pain and acknowledging it as something that is real. And that's problematic. And even again, in these rare disorders, not every single person is going to have pain. So when a patient has pain, we want to believe it and appreciate that it's real. About 8% of individuals have disabling pain. So their pain becomes something that can change their identity and can shape their day to day. It can be associated with hopelessness, apathy. Patients get demoralized by going from doctor to doctor. They can feel fatigued and start to lose interest in things. And then with that becomes this web of other challenges ranging from disability, isolating oneself from friends or family or coworkers. Sometimes starting to develop dependence on substances like opioids or benzodiazepines. Certainly having sleep problems is so common with chronic pain. And then sometimes patients turn towards risky behaviors or just not seeking medical care because they've given up. So when patients come to see me, they're coming to see a psychiatrist and they're always asking, does that mean I'm crazy? And the answer is no. It means again that your brain and your body are connected. And I'm going to work with patients to understand how to manage their pain. In order to untangle that intense web of comorbidity and psychosocial stressors, kind of start with a hypothesis. What are we trying to do to help manage your pain? And to break down that complexity, kind of try to simplify it to we know that damaged nerves produce aberrant signals and that aberrant signal is chronic pain. So we want to identify that pain because, again, just because you have a rare disease doesn't mean you should have pain. And so I'm going to provide a workup to try to understand why that person has pain. When Heidi saw me, I also kind of sent her through a similar workup. We're trying to understand, is there some type of small fiber neuropathy contributing to a pain? Is there something happening with the enteric nervous system or our gut that could be contributing to pain? What is it about the neurological exam or nerve conduction studies that can contribute to a diagnosis of pain? If patients have an autonomic nervous system dysfunction or concern of that, send them for a tilt table test and then look for various immune markers or autoimmune conditions or endocrine dysfunction that could be contributing and driving that chronic pain. With that workup, I'm able to make a diagnostic formulation. Sometimes those results don't really yield much. And I have to explain to a patient, well, I think your nerves are damaged. In Heidi's case, it's a little bit easier. We know her nerves are damaged. And so we can work on then trying to figure out how we can help those nerves heal. And so here's where the end of one trial comes into clinical care. There's no algorithm or textbook to be able to manage chronic pain for each individual. And so we have to do trial and errors of different medications using the knowledge we have in the pharmacology of the different medications. And certainly we've been through a lot of trial and errors in Heidi's case in terms of managing her pain. But with those errors or with those side effects, sometimes there are small victories. And sometimes that helps inform maybe what the pathology is that's driving the pain. We're also talking a lot about rehabilitation. So both physical rehabilitation and social engagement as well as psychological rehabilitation. And then the treatment involves both pharmacology and behavioral goals. And so pharmacologic, we're working on reducing that pathological process of the damaged nerves and working on reversing any associated conditions. So things like insomnia or depression. If a patient comes to see me, one of the first goals that we're going to talk about is that we're going to be working on enhancing their quality of life, supporting longevity, improving function. So we're really thinking about that quality of life. And again, treatment is going to be focusing on how are we going to try to help heal these damaged nerves. So one is to try to improve the signal to noise ratio of damaged nerves. So it's tend to think of we all have a smoke detector in the house and I'm short and it always seems to go away when no one else is in the house. Right. But then you find the battery, you replace the smoke detector and the thing still chirps. That's how I think about chronic pain is even though maybe the initial injury is there, the body keeps warning itself of danger, keeps sending signals. So we're trying to tune down those signals. We can use medications that help stabilize nerve membranes as well as physical activity to help tune down that process. We're also trying to amplify signals that might be worsening the pain. So if your autonomic nervous system is overactive, that can drive pain. So we're trying to use approaches to reduce that. A lot of patients have have depression or anxiety associated with their pain. So I'm focusing on helping to treat that depression as well. And then decreasing any type of noxious sensation or things that when individuals can really focus on that pain. And then again, trying to focus on ways to reduce any of the underlying nerve damage. And so sometimes that's trying to suppress the immune system, improving diet, improving the inflammatory processes. A lot of times patients say, you want to put me on so many medications or I'm on so many meds. And the reality is that we know now that chronic pain can't be treated with just one medication. It's a multimodal approach that can be at times successful. So in Heidi's case, I work on trying to add one medicine and then trying to maximize that medicine. But I also might be trying to add some other medicines and other modalities as well. So we can try to tackle pain from all these different pathways. So as we're going through treatment, it's important to realize that pain is a real disease. I actually had a patient just this week who said, is my pain fake? I said, no, you're experiencing your pain. That is real. And so it is a very subjective experience. And sometimes it really is invisible because we can't find the source of pain. But that doesn't take away the reality that that pain is there. And I try to help patients explain your nerves are damaged. Even if we can't find that exact nerve and our technology is not there yet, we understand that the nerves are damaged. The good news is that we can modulate our nervous system. And so there are ways that we can seek treatments. Depression is a treatable disease. And antidepressants can help modulate nerves and reduce pain. They can do that even if you don't have depression and more so when you do have depression. And importantly, physical activity is something that can really help improve the signal to noise ratio. The number one order on my EPIC set is referral to physical therapy. It's the first thing that always comes up. Because so many of my patients, I'm asking them to go to physical therapy. Again, not because I don't believe their pain is real or because we can't treat other ways, but it's so important to be physically active every single day. It has a long history. So it began, I think, in the 30s and 40s, understanding that you even was a theoretical concept when we understood what DNA inherited disease is, and then accumulated some accomplishments over the years, so that in the 80s and early 90s, we were finally able to deliver targeted gene therapies for rare disease. And over the past 40 years now, there's been an advancement in this field so that we have now many FDA approved therapies for these diseases. And I want to drill down a little bit on the first therapies because I think that they're important and they had a big impact on the way that things evolved over time. So the first successful gene therapy was for a four-year-old little girl, Ashanti De Silva, with severe combined immune deficiency. And this was a therapy where they took her T cells out, introduced the gene that was missing so that she could make that functional protein in her T cells, and then they re-infused them back into her. And it was really the first proof of concept that gene therapy could work. Building on that later, an additional trial involved a patient, Jesse Gelsinger, which I think is a very common name that we all know, an 18-year-old with ornithine transcarbomylase deficiency syndrome. And he was treated in 1999 with an AAV, an adenoviral vector gene therapy, and afterwards developed a really severe immune response that ultimately resulted in his death. And when that happened, it really prompted everyone to take a step back and put the brakes a lot on the development of gene therapies, and also prompted some major regulatory reforms and inclusion of safety practices in gene therapy that really hadn't been there to begin with and are continuing to evolve today. So what are gene therapies? So a gene therapy in a general sort of way is just that you provide a gene to a person who is deficient in that gene, and that gene enters the body into the cell and then creates the protein that that person is missing. And the goal is for it to have sort of a at least long lasting effect, but ideally a permanent effect on the disease force. And there are two major categories of gene therapies. So there's in vivo, which is a direct gene therapy. This is given usually IV or directly into an organ that is deficient in that in that protein's activity. And it is delivered in a carrier or a vector, which is usually a viral but can also be a non viral vector. And it gets into the target cells, and then corrects the protein deficit that is there. There are a couple examples of those for SMA and Duchenne muscular dystrophy. Ax vivo or cell based gene therapies. For those you take out the cells, so they're usually transforming blood cells or immune cells out of the body. And then you modify those cells genetically and return them to the body. And those can either be autologous, meaning you take the patient's blood out or the cells out, transform them, put them back in. Or they can be allergenic, which is from a donor. And there's a couple examples of those that have been approved also. So we talked about gene therapy, which is introduction of DNA material into the cells, right, in vivo and ex vivo. There are also a whole slew of nucleic acid based therapies that are not targeting like a permanent change in the genome, but are introducing some sort of genetic material that transiently modifies protein expression in a cell. And so we'll talk a little bit later about some some antisensitably gonucleotide therapies. But there's multiple mechanisms for those. We're not going to focus on those today. We're really just focusing on on the gene therapies, and in particular on the in vivo gene therapies. So I think a lot of you already know this, but just just to give a sense of how gene therapies are developed and delivered, you can think of it as as a truck that's delivering a package to a cell, right? So the truck essentially is the vector, it's the vehicle. So you take a virus and you take out kind of the pathogenic parts of it, parts that would cause a cold or other symptoms. But you keep the parts of that vector that allow it to get into the cell. Okay, then you load into that a version of the of the gene that you want expressed. Okay, and that is the package, right? That's product that you want inside the cell. On that package is a label, which is the promoter and and this is becoming increasingly sophisticated, but we can now add a promoter to that genetic material that directs its expression in the specific tissue that we want to see transformed. And as they are coming out, they're getting increasingly crazier names. Right? And I'm not going to try to say a lot of them. But in general, when you're thinking about gene therapies, you're thinking about the layers right of their mechanism. Right? So are we adding a gene? Are we editing a gene? Right? How are we delivering it? Is it in vivo? We give it directly to the patient? Are we treating cells outside the body in ex vivo sort of way? What is the vector? This slide gives a very high level overview of the major barriers and challenges that come with gene therapy. The barriers and challenges are grouped into three categories, clinical risks, psychosocial barriers and financial barriers. The goal here is to show that while gene therapy is incredibly promising, the real world implementation is very complex and multi layered. All of these barriers and challenges highlight why gene therapy requires patient careful patient selection, pre treatment evaluation and structured follow up. Thank you Sarah. ability. These therapies can slow disease progression but cannot repair damage that has already occurred. A major challenge is the presence of pre existing antibodies against the viral vectors. These antibodies may develop through prior exposure to naturally occurring viruses that resemble the vector or through earlier treatment with viral vector gene therapies. The presence of pre existing antibodies may exclude patients from treatment with certain gene therapies. 8av based gene therapies have been incredibly important in rare diseases, but they also come with specific vector related clinical risks that require very close monitoring. One category is infusion related reactions. Even though AAV vectors are modified so they cannot replicate the immune system can still recognize it. Some patients may develop fever, nausea, or other symptoms during or shortly after their infusion. Another major risk is hepatotoxicity. Because the liver is highly efficient at clearing viruses from the bloodstream, it receives the greatest exposure to the vector which can lead to inflammation. A much rarer but serious complication is thrombotic micro angiopathy or TMA. This involves small vessel injury and can affect the kidneys, blood counts, and overall health. It's uncommon but because it's very severe, we screen patients carefully and monitor them and their labs very closely after treatment. This is the vector related risk slide. And then now we'll transition from this broad overview into a case example. We'll look more closely at these barriers and challenges with Alevitis, which is a one time intravenous AAV mediated gene therapy for Duchenne muscular dystrophy. Duchenne is a degenerative muscle disease that can lead to cardiac complications and early death. We now have the transgene related risks. So we're shifting from the viral vector itself to the transgene, which is the therapeutic gene that we're delivering. In Alevitis, the transgene encodes micro dystrophin, a shortened version of the dystrophin protein. Many children with Duchenne produce little to no functional dystrophin. So micro dystrophin can be completely new to their immune system. When the body encounters a protein it has never seen before. It may identify it as foreign and mounts an immune response. The promoter used in Alevitis is designed to target skeletal and cardiac muscle, which are the two tissues most affected in Duchenne. Because those tissues are the main sites of expression. They are also the primary locations where immune reactions can occur. These immune responses can cause inflammation in the muscle or affect heart function. To reduce both vector related and transgene related risks, we use immunosuppressive medications such as steroids, but ongoing follow up remains essential to keep these patients safe. Looking at the general timeline of adverse events for Alevitis, most gastrointestinal events and thrombocytopenia or low platelets occur within the first two weeks. Cardiac events tend to appear within the first month and liver and muscle events are more likely to emerge between weeks four and eight. This timeline helps us to structure our monitoring and guide families on what to expect. I'm going to jump. As you can imagine, the monitor, the medical monitoring is only one part of the gene therapy journey. And the psychosocial impact on families is significant. For the first three months, families are coming to the hospital at least one to two times per week for testing. They're also giving their child a daily medication that they probably don't want to take. And they're managing frequent labs and other tasks. If concerns arise, families are expected to come in on it unexpectedly. Um, and for unplanned visits or testing on short notice, which adds even more disrupting to their lives. They take on real financial and practical burdens, including travel costs, parking fees at every visit and often unpaid time away from work. Their kids will miss school and time with their friends, which can make them feel very isolated. Families who aren't local may even need to relocate during that high risk period, which adds more strain. And on top of all of that, there's the emotional weight of receiving a complex intensive treatment. Patients and families are hopeful, but they're also anxious about the risks. Uncertain about the outcomes. And they have the responsibility of adhering to a strict monitoring plan. And that combination creates real pressure. Part of implementing gene therapy safely is recognizing these psychosocial barriers and making sure families have the support, education and resources they need to navigate this process. For the FDA approval process for cell and gene therapies. They are regulated under the FDA Center for Biologics Evaluation and Research otherwise known as CBER. This is the same agency of the FDA that approves biologic drugs and vaccines. Many of you may be familiar with the drug approval steps, but just to be well rounded here, wanted to quickly go over that. So essentially first manufacturer drug company has to develop a drug in the lab. That stuff alone can take several years decades, but once they feel that they're ready to test in humans, they need to get special permission from the FDA through an IND and then can start their clinical trials. From there, they then perform their clinical trials usually phases one, two, and three. But to note for some rare diseases, they actually just have to go to phase two to be considered for an FDA approval, because the rare disease patient populations can be so small. Once that is complete and the drug company is ready to file for review for the FDA, they then submit what's called a biologics license application or a BLA. So you may see this in the news. It's typically how it's abbreviated about a drug company filing their application. And that's where they send their very lengthy packet of information from their clinical trials, research and all of that, and asking for the FDA to review and ultimately approve the drug. And then at that point, the FDA reviews and makes their decision. Usually it takes about a year. But what's interesting, especially in the rare disease space is that there are now several accelerated approval pathways that the FDA will grant a manufacturer. And now there's a couple new programs, too. So even from early development stages, the drug company can get these special designations that will help speed up the clinical trials and then the review once they submit that BLA. I believe for the traditional accelerated approval pathway is what it's called. It's about a six month review as compared to 10 months to a year. And some may even be shorter windows than that moving forward, which is really interesting. === FILE: Rethinking PCOS： Early Identification, Lifelong Risks, and Opportunities for Prevention [xSJH6l31_u8].txt ===

 spenziologics, spenziologics, spenziologics, spenziologics, spenziologics, spenziologics, Hi, my name is Dr. Paradiso Sainzadeh. I'm a double board certified reproductive endocrinologist and obstetrician gynecologist, and I'm an assistant professor in the Department of Gynecology and Obstetrics at Johns Hopkins University. I also serve as the director of the PCOS Center. Today, I want to challenge how we think about PCOS and explore how earlier recognition and prevention-focused care could change the trajectory of health for millions of women. PCOS is the most common endocrine disorder in reproductive age women and affects about 5-10% of women globally. Despite its prevalence, it's frequently underdiagnosed or diagnosed years after symptoms begin. Traditionally, PCOS has been viewed primarily as a fertility disorder. However, we now understand that it's a lifelong metabolic and endocrine condition with implications far beyond reproduction. Many patients begin experiencing symptoms of PCOS during adolescence or early adulthood. These may include irregular or absent periods, acne, excess hair growth, or difficulty maintaining weight. Unfortunately, these symptoms are often attributed to stress, puberty, or lifestyle factors. Because of this, diagnosis may be delayed by several years. The most widely used diagnostic framework is the Rotterdam criteria. Based on the Rotterdam criteria, we diagnose PCOS if someone has two of the three following criteria. With number one being irregular cycles or having ovulatory dysfunction. Number two, if someone's presenting with symptoms of elevated androgens, including facial hair growth or acne, or having elevated androgens on their blood check. Third criteria is having polycystic appearing ovaries on the ultrasound. So, as I mentioned, we diagnose PCOS if someone has two of these three criteria, but that is if we have excluded all other causes, including thyroid disorders, adrenal gland disorders, some pituitary disorders, and also androgen producing cysts of the ovary. It is so important to know that PCOS is a heterogeneous condition, meaning patients may present with different combinations of this symptom. The biology of PCOS involves multiple interacting pathways. insulin resistance and hyperinsulinemia play a central role contributing to increased androgen production and disruptions in hypothalamic pituitary ovarian signaling. These pathways reinforce each other and help explain the chronic nature of PCOS. So, PCOS is not just a reproductive disorder. Patients face higher risk of type 2 diabetes, metabolic syndrome, dyslipidemia, and early onset cardiovascular disease. They also have an increased risk of endometrial hyperplasia and cancer of the uterus due to chronic inovulation. Recognizing PCOS early provides an opportunity to address these risks. One of the most important opportunities in PCOS care is prevention. Early diagnosis allows early intervention, which includes lifestyle modification and optimization, such as healthy nutrition, regular physical activity, and weight management. Lifestyle modifications are still the cornerstone of PCOS treatment. Even a small weight loss of approximately 5-10% of body weight can significantly improve metabolic and reproductive outcomes. We have medications and medical therapies that can address both metabolic and reproductive aspects of PCOS, one of which include metformin. Metformin is commonly known as a diabetes medication, and it can improve insulin sensitivity and metabolic parameters and results in regular ovulation in some women. Hormonal therapies, such as the combined oral contraceptives or progesterone-only measures, can be used to regulate menstrual cycles and protect the endometrium. Antiandrogen therapies help treat hirsutism and acne. More recently, the GLP-1 receptor agonists have shown promise in the PCOS population to help with weight management, metabolic optimization, and resuming regular cycles. PCOS also affects mental health. Patients experience higher rates of anxiety, depression, and body image concerns. Stigma around symptoms, such as weight gain or excess heart growth may delay care seeking. Comprehensive PCOS care must include attention to emotional well-being. PCOS should be viewed as a lifelong condition rather than a temporary reproductive issue. Management should focus on metabolic health, reproductive planning, and preventive care. A multidisciplinary approach can help patients maintain health throughout their lifespan. This is the prevention framework of PCOS. As I mentioned, early recognition helps with early introduction of lifestyle optimization measures. And then these patients will be followed with metabolic monitoring, monitoring of their reproductive health. In summary, PCOS is a lifelong cardiometabolic condition. Early identification allows us to intervene earlier and reduce long-term health risks. By focusing on prevention and delivering multidisciplinary integrated care, we can improve both metabolic and reproductive outcomes. The earlier we recognize PCOS, the more lives we can change. In summary, PCOS is a lifelong cardiometabolic disease. The earlier we diagnose the disease, the earlier we are able to intervene and help reduce the long-term health risks. Here at the Johns Hopkins Multidisciplinary PCOS Center, we specialize in providing multifaceted care to your patients with PCOS. So for left-sided diaphragmatic hernias, it needs to be an isolated diaphragmatic hernia without any additional physical abnormalities and genetic abnormalities with a lung size that's less than 30% than expected. And for a right-sided diaphragmatic hernia, it's the same basic criteria, but a lung size smaller than 45%. And both of these hernia types, so right and left, are now eligible for fetal therapy. So fetal therapy for diaphragmatic hernia is done by a procedure that's called fetoscopic tracheal occlusion. This is a procedure where we go with a small camera into the uterus, into the windpipe of the baby, and put a small balloon in place that's inflated for a period about six weeks or so. During that period of time, the lung fluid that's normally produced cannot escape, and the backward pressure reverses some of the compression effects on the lung. The treatment consists of placing the balloon, observing the lung growth that you see, and then removing the balloon. And then the baby can deliver vaginally and gets the regular treatment afterwards, with a hope that the balloon occlusion has improved the overall lung function and outcome at the time of delivery. The risks of fetal therapy are related to that there is an intervention required to place the balloon in the trachea. So anytime you enter the uterus, there's a risk of preterm labor, rupture of membranes. There's the risk that the patient might go into preterm labor while the balloon is still in place. And if there needs to preterm delivery, then you need to have an emergency setup to really emergently remove the balloon. And after you remove the balloon, the baby still benefits from a period of maturation until term. So preterm birth is always bad for a baby with diaphragmatic hernia. So that is really one of the biggest adverse effects of this treatment. In terms of additional risks, well, there is also the risk that the balloon doesn't produce as much benefit as you would have hoped for. And all of these things are important considerations that we discuss with our parents. At the Johns Hopkins Center for Fetal Therapy, we have particularly emphasized the need to manage the risk for preterm labor and preterm birth. And we have achieved consistently over the last seven or eight years, a very good safety profile for fetal. Our patients deliver on average at 39 weeks, which is on average five weeks later than what is published in the literature. And this is an important advantage for our patients because they don't just have the benefit of fetal, but the baby also has the benefit of additional maturation and essentially be born as a term baby rather than a preterm baby. The standard treatment of CDH is operation, operative repair after delivery. When you compare babies that have similar severity of diaphragmatic hernia, and you compare the group that has received fetal or just standard postnatal care without fetal. The babies on fetal or that have received fetal seem to require less intensive care support to remain oxygenated. So the first 48 hours of transition are very important for a neonate with CDH. So that is how we participate. And we will stay in contact with our pediatric colleagues until the babies are typically discharged. That means we have a very engaged social work team. We have nursing team. We have access to resources. And we do a personalized assessment of all families to see what is important for families, what support they may need. So our social worker actually meets with everybody. And we sort of make a needs assessment and see how we can best help our families. That can take the form of transportation support, accommodation. We have a good relationship with some of the local resources here. And also for babies that have a likely very difficult course after delivery, we also have access to palliative care and chaplain support for emotional and sort of spiritual help, which can be important for families as well. So we really try to tailor our support services to the needs of the family. We also have a very engaged parent advisory council that has a peer-to-peer program where our patients can get in contact with prior patients that were treated at the center. And they went through the journey of fetal treatment or standard treatment of CDH and then raising a child. Many of the families that we've seen have children that are 7, 8, 9, 10 years old now that hadn't very severe diaphragmatic hernias. And that can be very helpful for families to get that perspective.

Typically, pap smears are a test to screen for cervical cancer and so they actually do not need to be performed every year if your previous pap smear was normal within a certain duration of time. If your pap smear is abnormal, however, that recommendation can change. So the pap smear itself basically screens for changes to the cell architecture on the cervix. So we basically look to see how are the cells on the cervix changing from what their normal formation or architecture should look like. If there's no change, that's great, that means it's normal. And if there is change, we categorize that in terms of how far away they are from the normal architecture, so low or high grade or unknown. Now, for patients under age 30, so my patients in their 20s, we don't routinely check for HPV unless the cells are abnormal. And the reason for that is that often under age 30, our bodies are really able to fight back HPV and can often basically get rid of the infection on their own. Now, after age 30, our immune systems are a little bit less good at that. And so that's why we end up testing for it more often. Based on the results of that pap smear on the cytology, which is the cell architecture, and on the HPV testing, if you're over age 30, we determine how frequently you need any pap smear is going forward. Now, how can you prevent cervical cancer? We touched very briefly on HPV. HPV is the number one cause of cervical cancer. It causes the cells on the cervix to change. We know that 80 to 90% of sexually active people will eventually get HPV. And like I talked about earlier, most people will have the immune system to be able to combat that. However, as we get older, and HPV can hang around longer, it has more time to basically start changing the architecture of those cells. One way to protect yourself from HPV is to use condoms and, you know, be careful in having intercourse with other people. But the other is also to get the HPV vaccine. And this is really the best tool we have against cervical cancer. The HPV vaccine covers the highest risk strains of the virus and essentially protects you from the ones that are most likely to cause cervical cancer. And depending on a person's behavior or sexual behavior, we may actually recommend getting it later in life. I personally recommend that anyone who hasn't received the vaccine and is eligible for it should strongly consider getting it because it is a cancer preventing vaccine. These data may not quite be extrapolated to vaping. I think I would have to look up that information, but I did get asked that recently. Typically when you are younger, so when you get it in that 11 or 12-year age window, it's a two-shot series. As an adult, in order to get the vaccine to really stick in our immune systems, it's actually a three-shot series. And they're typically several months apart. So you get it once, then you get it two months later, then you get it four months after the second shot. So that's three shots. Once you've done that whole series, you do not need to get it again. Occasionally, there have been some folks when they test for HPV and test positive that will consider getting another booster of it. There's not necessarily data to say that that's helpful, but certainly could be considered. Question number three. Can an OBGYN serve as a primary care provider for women, or is it important to have a separate PCP for general health needs? This is an excellent question. So I usually talk to patients about this because we do a really great job at managing most gynecologic primary issues. So we kind of talked about pap smear, breast cancer screenings, all kinds of things. And in your early 20s and 30s, we certainly can be a good source of care as a primary person to be managing you. However, as we get older and we develop things like hypertension, diabetes, weight issues, things like that, the gynecologist may not be the best person to manage your primary care conditions because we're not experts in every single aspect of health, although we certainly try to be. We are truly women's health and really reproductive health experts is how I would frame it. So sometimes I'll joke that I'm a below the belt doctor. So I do recommend very strongly that you have a primary care doctor to help manage other conditions. And often we actually work together primary care doctors and gynecologists to ensure that you have a very seamless care team and that you have a really good outcome for both things like high blood pressure, diabetes, no vaccinations, overall screenings, and then of course your reproductive health as they go hand in hand. Number four, I'm 41. Is it too late to think about freezing my eggs? This is a really good question. I think that it's best to talk to a fertility specialist when deciding to freeze your eggs. In general, the quantity and quality of our eggs decreases significantly after 35 and exponentially after age 40. Typically, a fertility specialist will do an assessment for each individual to see if there is an opportunity there to stimulate those ovaries and make them produce as many eggs as we can in order to retrieve them for preservation or egg freezing, if you will. And so I always recommend getting that evaluation done. If it's something that's really important to you and you hope to have children in the future, I say go for it. Go ahead and talk to them because you never know. You may be able to get a good outcome, but it's hard to guarantee, certainly. Number five, do we have to ask our doctor to do the HPV test with your pap smear? That is an excellent question. Typically your doctor will know that when your age guidelines are appropriate for HPV testing. If anyone, again, under age 30, HPV testing is reflex, meaning if your test is abnormal, the pathologist will then check for HPV to see if the HPV is what's causing that abnormality. And for patients over age 30, the HPV testing is routinely recommended and we routinely order that for you with your cytology, meaning with that cell assessment for the cervix. But you can always bring it up. It never hurts. All right. Number six, is it normal to experience more painful periods in your late 30s and is this a sign of perimenopause? Really good question. So generally speaking, perimenopause is typically the period leading up to menopause. For most women, this is in their mid to late 40s because the average age of menopause in the United States is 51. So thinking about that, I would say it's very unlikely for a young woman in their 30s to be experiencing perimenopause and heavy cycles is usually, heavier painful cycles are usually not the first indicator of that. That said, having a heavier painful cycle should warrant evaluation because it can indicate many things, including things like fibroids like we talked about. It can indicate things like adenomyosis. It could be even an ovarian cyst that could be causing some of that pain or discomfort during your cycle. And so it's best to see your gynecologist and get an exam. Number seven, do I need a referral from a gynecologist for a mammogram? Yes. So for your mammogram, you should absolutely see your gynecologist. Some primary care doctors will also offer that for you and that's appropriate as well. The mammogram screening starts at age 40. So we typically start doing breast exams for everybody every year that you see your gynecologist at your annual exam, just to be sure we're not missing anything. But the actual mammogram, which is that radiologic screening of the breast tissue, starts at age 40 at the earliest based on our screening guidelines. And typically it resumes every year essentially until about 75. So we have lots of time to get those mammograms done. That said, if you have a strong family history of breast cancer, meaning a first-degree relative, your mother, your grandmother, your sister who has had breast cancer before menopause, meaning before age 50, please share that with your doctor because that can actually indicate earlier screening for breast cancer. And they should know when to order that for you based on the age timeline for your family member. And also if your family has any history of genetic disorders like BRCA1 or 2, which put you at increased risk of breast cancer at a young age as well. Okay. Number 8, are there any long-term effects on fertility from using hormonal birth control for many years? No. This is a very common misconception online these days and it's not true. So generally speaking, your hormonal birth control is working for the duration of time that you were using it for. Again, hormonal birth control can vary significantly, right? It can look like the hormonal IUD, which is progesterone only, or the combined birth control pill, which is a daily medication, right? And so the duration of time that it works really depends on the formulation of the medication that you're on and does not have any impact on your long-term fertility. It works for the duration of the time that you're using it to prevent pregnancy so that you can safely defer your pregnancy for the future. That said, if you're using birth control all the way until you're age 40, it is not the birth control that was causing your infertility, but as we get older, the quality and quantity of our eggs decreases and so by the time you get to age 40, it would not be unexpected that your fertility has waned unrelated to whether you used a contraceptive or not. All right. And then we've reached our last question. Can lifestyle factors like stress or diet affect the regularity and symptoms of your menstrual cycle? Absolutely. So stress is certainly a big indicator of how the menstrual cycle can progress. We know that patients that experience extreme stress or duress can often find that their cycles are dysregulated, that they may have less reliable periods or more pain in general as related to other pain disorders as well, and that can certainly have an impact. The diet question is a little bit more difficult to assess. I would say less likely to impact menstrual cycle, but certainly can affect your symptoms. So if a food causes more significant bloating and you have it a lot during your cycle when you're already feeling bloating, that can certainly accentuate some of the symptoms that you may be experiencing from your menstrual cycle as well. But stress, we know absolutely can impact when you menstruate, how often, and so on and so forth. All right. Thank you guys all for coming. That was a really wonderful discussion.
